Convergence of Diffusion Models Under the Manifold Hypothesis in High-Dimensions
Iskander Azangulov, Judith Rousseau, George Deligiannidis

Abstract
Denoising Diffusion Probabilistic Models (DDPM) are powerful state-of-the-art methods used to generate synthetic data from high-dimensional data distributions and are widely used for image, audio, and video generation as well as many more applications in science and beyond. The manifold hypothesis states that high-dimensional data often lie on lower-dimensional manifolds within the ambient space, and is widely believed to hold in provided examples. While recent results have provided invaluable insight into how diffusion models adapt to the manifold hypothesis, they do not capture the great empirical success of these models, making this a very fruitful research direction. In this work, we study DDPMs under the manifold hypothesis and prove that they achieve rates independent of the ambient dimension in terms of score learning. In terms of sampling complexity, we obtain rates independent of the ambient dimension w.r.t. the Wasserstein distance. We do this by developing a new framework connecting diffusion models to the well-studied theory of extrema of Gaussian Processes.



\declaretheorem

[style=remark]Assumption

Keywords:
diffusion models, minimax, manifold learning.

1
Introduction

Generative models
[
40
]
represent a cornerstone of modern machine learning, tasked with the synthesis of new samples from an unknown distribution having only access to samples from it. Once trained, they are employed in various applications such as image and audio generation, natural language processing, and scientific simulations. Prominent examples include Generative Adversarial Networks (GANs), Variational Autoencoders (VAEs), and, more recently, Diffusion Models.

Diffusion Models
[
22
,
35
]
, also known as Denoising Diffusion Probabilistic Models (DDPM) or Score-based generative models, have drawn significant attention due to their robust theoretical foundation and exceptional performance in generating high-quality data
[
14
,
45
,
23
,
18
,
47
]
. They operate by simulating a stochastic process where data is first gradually corrupted with Gaussian noise and then reconstructed back, allowing the model to generate complex data distributions from white noise.

One of the most intriguing capabilities of diffusion models, supported with a lot of empirical and theoretical evidence
[
9
,
32
,
36
,
39
,
11
]
, is their efficiency in learning distributions supported on low-dimensional manifold structures. Even more surprising is that this happens even though diffusion models are solely defined in terms of the ambient space.

The manifold hypothesis, see e.g.
[
28
]
, postulates
that high-dimensional data often lie on lower-dimensional manifolds within the ambient
space and offers a framework to explain why complex data can often be represented or approximated using fewer degrees of freedom than their raw dimensionality suggests. Different approaches to incorporate the manifold hypothesis have been fruitfully studied in the literature
[
2
,
15
,
21
,
8
,
13
]
.

1.1
Related Works

Formally, we assume that we observe
n
samples from an unknown
\alpha
-smooth distribution
\mu
that is supported on an unknown
d
-dimensional,
\beta
-smooth manifold
M
, isometrically embedded into
\mathbb{R}^{D}
(see Section
2.2
for definitions). The concept of
minimax estimation
refers to finding an estimator that minimizes the worst-case error and plays an important role in statistical learning theory. The minimax rate of estimation of the unknown manifold
M
was studied by
[
2
]
, who proved that a local polynomial estimator achieves the minimax optimal rate
n^{-{\beta}/{d}}
up to a
\log
-factor.
This result was then adapted by
[
15
]
to construct a kernel-based minimax estimator
\hat{\mu}_{n}
of
\mu
which converges at the optimal rate
n^{-\left(\alpha+1\right)/\left(2\alpha+d\right)}
in the
W_{1}
metric. However, sampling from
\hat{\mu}_{n}
requires running a costly MCMC algorithm, thereby making it hard to use in practice.

In contrast, sampling from diffusion models is relatively fast, and the resulting samples are of high quality even in high-dimensional settings
[
14
]
. On a high level
[
47
]
, diffusion models are trained to reverse the
forward process
that gradually adds noise to the unknown distribution
\mu
. The resulting
reverse process
can be described as a Stochastic Differential Equation (SDE) given in terms of the
score function

s(t,x)
. More precisely, they first learn an approximation
\hat{s}(t,x)
to the true score, and then simulate the discretized SDE to get samples from an approximation
\hat{\mu}
of the true distribution
\mu
. Thereby two key quantities determining the performance of DDPMs are the accuracy of
\hat{s}(t,x)
, and the design of the discretization scheme.

In their seminal paper
[
31
]
obtain (near) minimax convergence rates
n^{-\left(\alpha+1\right)/\left(2\alpha+D\right)+\delta}
of diffusion models for any
\delta>0
under the Wasserstein-1 metric, assuming
\mu
has
\alpha
-smooth compactly supported density on
\mathbb{R}^{D}
and perfect SDE simulation. Simulateneously
[
11
]
obtained suboptimal convergence rates in total variation under a linear manifold hypothesis.

Recently,
[
39
]
generalized this result to the case when
\mu
is
\alpha
-smooth and supported on unknown compact
d
-dimensional manifold
M
. They showed that diffusion models achieve the Wasserstein convergence rate of order
D^{\alpha+d/2}n^{-(\alpha+1)/(2\alpha+d)}
, which is optimal in
n
but has a strong dependence on
D
via the term
D^{\alpha+d/2}
.
It is worth mentioning that the rate obtained in
[
15
]
is independent of the ambient dimension, raising the question of whether similar results are possible for DDPMs.

Since the first version of this work, several related results have appeared.

[
24
,
19
]
study diffusion models under low-dimensional factorization assumptions, showing that they can achieve minimax-optimal rates beyond the full ambient-dimensional setting.
Closer to the manifold framework,
[
46
]
relaxes the exact manifold assumption while retaining intrinsic-dimensional rates, and
[
37
]
gives an alternative generalization proof based on stability of the reverse dynamics and induced score regularity.

1.2
Our Contribution

The strong dependence on
D
of the bound
D^{\alpha+d/2}n^{-(\alpha+1)/(2\alpha+d)}
obtained by
[
39
]
does not allow a satisfactory explanation of the excellent empirical behavior of diffusion models in typical scenarios, when
D
is very large, possibly much larger than
n
. For instance if
D\gtrsim n
then the error becomes
n^{\alpha+d/2}n^{-(\alpha+1)/(2\alpha+d)}
which goes to infinity with
n
as soon as
d\geq 2
.
Therefore, in order to understand if the manifold hypothesis provides a reasonable explanation for the success of diffusion models, we need to derive sharper bounds in terms of
D
.

In this paper, we fill this gap by showing that the normalized score function
\sigma_{t}s(t,x)
can be learned by a neural network estimator with the (near) optimal convergence rate
n^{-\left(\alpha+1\right)/\left(2\alpha+d\right)}
up to
\polylog
term w.r.t. the score matching loss, implying an optimal (up to
\polylog
) rate
n^{-{(\alpha+1)}/\left(2\alpha+d\right)}
in the Wasserstein metric as long as
\log D=O\left(\log n\right)
. In other words the ambient dimension
D
only impacts the rate via a logarithmic term.

The backbone of both results is a novel high-probability bound on the score function depending only on the intrinsic dimension
d
. To obtain this bound, we leverage classic concentration results on the maximum of Gaussian processes and carefully study the interaction of the
D
-dimensional Gaussian noise added during the forward process with vectors lying on the
d
-dimensional manifold.

We show that the score function is sensitive only to the points around the denoised preimage, and as a result, the precision with which the score function points in the direction of the denoised point does not depend on the ambient dimension, demonstrating that diffusion models adapt well to the geometry of the manifold.

We slightly modify the manifold estimator given in
[
2
]
and construct a dimension-reduction scheme allowing us to use
n
samples to build an efficient approximation of a
\beta
-smooth manifold
M
, with error
n^{-\beta/d}
, by
n
polynomial surfaces each contained in easy-to-find sub-spaces of dimension
O(\log n)
.

Combining our high-probability bounds with this dimension reduction scheme, we improve the neural network architecture presented in
[
39
]
, thus obtaining the aforementioned ambient-dimension-free convergence bound
n^{-\left(\alpha+1\right)/\left(2\alpha+d\right)}\polylog(n)
on the score estimation.

Finally, by combining the discretization schemes presented in
[
31
]
and
[
33
]
, we prove that simulating the diffusion model with the presented score estimator for
n^{\frac{2\alpha+d}{\alpha+1}}\polylog n
steps achieves an error of
n^{-\frac{\alpha+1}{2\alpha+d}}\polylog n
. This allows us to derive an inequality on the Wasserstein distance between the simulations and the true generating process independent of the ambient dimension
D
.

In Section
2
, we introduce the notation and definitions related to diffusion models and manifold learning. In Section
3
we present our main results. In Sections
4
–
5
we present key steps in the control of the score matching loss to avoid the curse of dimensionality. In Section
7
we propose an alternative estimator of the score function based on an estimator of
\mu
proposed by
[
15
]
. In Section
6
, we present the discretization scheme achieving the minimax optimal
W_{1}
rate. Discussion is provided in Section
8
. Finally, the detailed proofs are provided in the appendices.

2
Preliminaries

2.1
Score-Matching Generative Models

Throughout the paper, we consider a data set
\mathcal{Y}=\{y_{1},\cdots,y_{n}\}
consisting of independent, identically distributed samples from
\mu
. Generative models aim at simulating new data from an approximation of
\mu
trained on
\mathcal{Y}
.
We follow the notation used in
[
31
]
to describe the methodology.

Let
\{B_{t}\}_{t\in[0,\overline{T}]}
denote a
D
-dimensional Brownian motion on
[0,\overline{T}]
. For a measure
\mu
supported on
\mathcal{X}\subset\mathbb{R}^{D}
, we consider the forward process
\{X_{t}\}_{t\in[0,\overline{T}]}
defined as a standard
D
-dimensional Ornstein–Ulhenbeck (OU) process with initial condition
X_{0}\sim\mu
, which is given as the solution of the SDE

\begin{cases}dX_{t}=-X_{t}dt+\sqrt{2}dB_{t},\\
X_{0}\sim\mu.\end{cases}

(2.1)

Let
Z_{D}\sim\mathcal{N}\left(0,\Id_{D}\right)
denote a standard
D
-dimensional random normal vector independent from
X_{0}
. It is well known that

X_{t}\mid X_{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle dist.}}{{=}}c_{t}X_{0}+\sigma_{t}Z_{D}\sim q_{t}(\cdot|X_{0}),

(2.2)

where
c_{t}:=e^{-t}
and
\sigma_{t}:=\sqrt{1-e^{-2t}}
and
q_{t}
is the transition density of the OU process.

We use
\mu_{t}
to denote the law of
X_{t}
and
p(t,\cdot)
to denote the (unnormalized) marginal density of
X_{t}
, i.e.

p(t,x):=\int_{\mathcal{X}}e^{-\|x-c_{t}y\|^{2}/2\sigma^{2}_{t}}\mu(dy).

(2.3)

Under mild conditions on
\mu
,

[
6
]
has shown that
the backward process
\{Y_{t}\}_{t\in[0,\overline{T}]}
defined as
Y_{t}=X_{\overline{T}-t}
solves the SDE

\begin{cases}dY_{t}=\left[Y_{t}+2\nabla\log p\left(\overline{T}-t,Y_{t}\right)\right]dt+\sqrt{2}dB_{t},\\
Y_{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle dist.}}{{=}}X_{\overline{T}}.\end{cases}

(2.4)

Notice that using (
2.3
), the score function
\nabla\log p(t,x)
can be expressed in terms of
\mu
as

s(t,x)=\nabla\log p(t,x)=\frac{\nabla p(t,x)}{p(t,x)}=\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}_{t}}\frac{\int_{\mathcal{X}}(c_{t}y-x)e^{-\|x-c_{t}y\|^{2}/2\sigma^{2}_{t}}\mu(dy)}{\int_{\mathcal{X}}e^{-\|x-c_{t}y\|^{2}/2\sigma^{2}_{t}}\mu(dy)}.

(2.5)

By construction,
Y_{\overline{T}}=X_{0}\sim\mu
; in other words, one can obtain a sample from
\mu
by first sampling from
Y_{0}=X_{\overline{T}}
, and then simulating the backward dynamics given in (
2.4
). This scheme, however, is not feasible in practice as it requires access to samples from
X_{\overline{T}}
, to the true score function
s(t,x)=\nabla\log p(t,x)
and to be able to solve (
2.4
).

The main idea behind generative score-matching models is to simulate the sampling procedure just described approximately. Firstly, since
X_{\overline{T}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle dist.}}{{\rightarrow}}\mathcal{N}\left(0,\Id_{D}\right)
as
\overline{T}
goes to infinity, at a dimension-free rate, we can approximate
Y_{0}=X_{\overline{T}}
by
\hat{Y}_{0}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\Id_{D})
. Secondly, the true score
s(t,x)
is replaced with an approximation
\hat{s}(t,x)
learned using data
\mathcal{Y}
, and the backward dynamics (
2.4
) is replaced by an appropriate discretization of the process

\begin{cases}d\hat{Y}_{t}=\left[\hat{Y}_{t}+2\hat{s}\left(\overline{T}-t,\hat{Y}_{t}\right)\right]dt+\sqrt{2}B_{t},\\
\hat{Y}_{0}\sim\mathcal{N}\left(0,\Id_{D}\right).\end{cases}

(2.6)

Let
\hat{\mu}_{t}:=\textsf{Law}(\hat{Y}_{\overline{T}-t})
denote the law of the obtained samples. A common practice is to use early stopping to avoid the numerical problems with the score function
s(t,x)
that emerge when
t=0
. Early stopping entails stopping the simulation early at a time
\overline{T}-\underline{T}
for a small
\underline{T}>0
and uses
\hat{\mu}_{\underline{T}}
to approximate
\mu
.

The approximation
\hat{s}(t,x)
is learned by minimization of the empirical version of the score matching loss which depends on
\underline{T}<\overline{T}
and is defined as

\int_{\underline{T}}^{\overline{T}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{D}}\|\hat{s}(t,x)-s(t,x)\|^{2}p(t,x)dtdx=\int_{\underline{T}}^{\overline{T}}\mathbb{E}\|\hat{s}\left(t,X_{t}\right)-s\left(t,X_{t}\right)\|^{2}dt.

(2.7)

The score matching loss controls
[
31
, Eq. (90)]
the Wasserstein loss between the target
\mu
and the approximation
\hat{\mu}_{\underline{T}}
. More precisely, for
T_{0}=\underline{T}<T_{1}<T_{2}<\ldots T_{K}=\overline{T}
, and any
\delta>0

W_{1}\left(\hat{\mu},\mu\right)\lesssim\sqrt{D}\left(\sqrt{\underline{T}}+\sum_{k=0}^{K-1}\sqrt{\log\delta^{-1}\cdot\sigma^{2}_{T_{k+1}}\int_{T_{k}}^{T_{k+1}}\mathbb{E}\|\hat{s}\left(t,X_{t}\right)-s\left(t,X_{t}\right)\|^{2}dt}+\delta+e^{-\overline{T}}\right).

(2.8)

Remark
.

Eq. (90) in
[
31
]
doesn’t address the dependence on the ambient dimension
D
, however, it can be easily deduced from the proof of Lemma D.7.

Another metric that is often used to assess the performance of Score-matching Generative models is the
\KL
distance between
\hat{Y}_{\overline{T}-\underline{T}}
and
X_{\underline{T}}
. More precisely
[
12
, Section 5.2]
,
if
\mu
has finite second moments

D_{\KL}\left(\hat{\mu}_{\underline{T}}\|\mu_{\underline{T}}\right)\lesssim De^{-2\overline{T}}+\int_{\underline{T}}^{\overline{T}}\mathbb{E}\|\hat{s}\left(t,X_{t}\right)-s\left(t,X_{t}\right)\|^{2}dt,

(2.9)

where we recall that
\hat{\mu}_{\underline{T}},\mu_{\underline{T}}
are distributions of
\hat{Y}_{\overline{T}-\underline{T}}
and
X_{\underline{T}}
.

Since the computation of the loss function (
2.7
) requires access to the true score
s(t,x)
, the equivalent denoising score matching loss is used instead during the optimization step.

[
43
]
showed that there is a constant
C_{\mu}
that does not depend on
\hat{s}
such that

\int_{\underline{T}}^{\overline{T}}\mathbb{E}\|\hat{s}\left(t,X_{t}\right)-s\left(t,X_{t}\right)\|^{2}dt=\int_{\underline{T}}^{\overline{T}}\mathbb{E}_{X_{0}\sim\mu}\mathbb{E}_{Z_{D}}\|\hat{s}\left(t,c_{t}X_{0}+\sigma_{t}Z_{D}\right)+Z_{D}/\sigma_{t}\|^{2}dt+C_{\mu},

(2.10)

Hence defining for
y\in M
the loss function
\ell_{y}
as

\ell_{y}(\hat{s},a,b):=\int_{a}^{b}\mathbb{E}_{Z_{D}}\|\hat{s}\left(t,c_{t}y+\sigma_{t}Z_{D}\right)+Z_{D}/\sigma_{t}\|^{2}dt,

(2.11)

it is enough to control

\mathcal{R}(\hat{s},a,b)=\mathbb{E}_{y\sim\mu}\ell_{y}(\hat{s},a,b)=\int_{a}^{b}\mathbb{E}\|\hat{s}\left(t,X_{t}\right)-s\left(t,X_{t}\right)\|^{2}dt-C_{\mu},

(2.12)

for all
a=T_{k},b=T_{k+1}
and
k\leq K
.
Finally, we define the empirical risk
\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{Y}}(\hat{s},a,b)
w.r.t. samples
\mathcal{Y}=\{y_{1},\ldots,y_{n}\}
as

\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{Y}}(\hat{s},a,b)=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\ell_{y_{i}}(\hat{s},a,b).

(2.13)

Tweedie’s formula
[
34
]
offers an alternative, very useful, interpretation of the score
s(t,x)
as the expected value of the noise added to get
x
.

s(t,x)=\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}_{t}}\frac{\int_{M}\left(c_{t}y-x\right)e^{-\left\lVert x-c_{t}y\right\rVert^{2}/2\sigma^{2}_{t}}\mu(dy)}{\int_{M}e^{-\left\lVert x-c_{t}y\right\rVert^{2}/2\sigma^{2}_{t}}\mu(dy)}=\int_{M}\frac{c_{t}y-x}{\sigma^{2}_{t}}\mu(dy|t,x)dy=-\sigma^{-1}_{t}\mathbb{E}\left(Z_{D}\mid X_{t}=x\right),

(2.14)

where
\mu(dy|t,x)
is defined as the probability measure proportional to
e^{-\left\lVert x-c_{t}y\right\rVert^{2}/2\sigma^{2}_{t}}\mu(dy)
.

By Bayes rule
\mu(dy|t,x)
is the conditional law of
\left(X_{0}\big|X_{t}=x\right)
. We will denote the corresponding conditional expectation as

e(t,x):=\mathbb{E}\left[X_{0}\big|X_{t}=x\right]=\frac{\int_{M}y\cdot e^{-\left\lVert x-c_{t}y\right\rVert^{2}/2\sigma^{2}_{t}}\mu(dy)}{\int_{M}e^{-\left\lVert x-c_{t}y\right\rVert^{2}/2\sigma^{2}_{t}}\mu(dy)},

(2.15)

substituting into (
2.5
), we represent the score
s(t,x)
as

s(t,x)=\frac{c_{t}}{\sigma^{2}_{t}}e(t,x)-\frac{x}{\sigma^{2}_{t}}.

(2.16)

2.2
Manifold Hypothesis

In this paper,
we study the behavior of score-matching generative models
under the manifold assumption, i.e. we assume that the support
M
of
\mu
is a low dimensional manifold. The manifold hypothesis is particularly relevant as a way to understand the behavior of statistical or learning algorithms in high dimensions; see for instance
[
15
]
who showed that under the manifold hypothesis, it is possible to construct estimators of
\mu
whose Wasserstein distance to
\mu
is independent of the ambient dimension
D
. Whether this is feasible in the context of score-matching diffusion models has been an open question so far.

We mainly follow
[
15
]
to define the class of regular manifolds that we will study. Throughout the paper we denote the distance between a point
x
and a set
M
as
\dist(x,M)=\inf_{y\in M}\|x-y\|
, the ball in
\mathbb{R}^{k}
centered at
x
with radius
r
, for
k\geq 1,x\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
and
r>0
, as
B_{k}(x,r)
and more generally we write
B_{\mathcal{U}}(x,r)
for the ball in a space
\mathcal{U}
. Moreover for
f:\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\mapsto\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}
, we write
d^{i}f(x)
for the
i
-th differential of
f
at
x
and for
k\in\mathbb{N}
, the
k
-th Hölder norm of
f
, when it exists, is denoted by

\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{C^{\alpha}(\Omega)}=\max_{0\leq i\leq k}\sup_{x\in\Omega}\left\lVert d^{i}f(x)\right\rVert_{op}
,
where
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{op}
denotes the operator norm.

The key quantity, first introduced in
[
20
]
, determining the manifold’s regularity is called the
reach

\tau
and is defined as

\displaystyle\tau

\displaystyle:=\sup\{\varepsilon\big|\forall x\in M^{\varepsilon}\,\,\,\,\exists!\,y\in M,\text{ s.t. }\dist(x,M)=\|x-y\|\},

\displaystyle\text{where }M^{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{D}:\dist(x,M)<\varepsilon\}.

Throughout the paper we assume that the reach is bounded from below by
\tau>\tau_{\min}
; we refer to
[
2
]
for a detailed discussion about the necessity of this assumption.

The smoothness of the manifold is determined in terms of the smoothness of local charts
[
26
]
; when the manifold is embedded into
\mathbb{R}^{D}
this means that for all
y\in M
the manifold can be locally represented as a graph of a
\beta
-smooth one-to-one function
\Phi_{y}:U_{y}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}\mapsto\mathbb{R}^{D}
, where
0\in U_{y}
is an open set and
\Phi_{y}(0)=y
. If this holds we say that the manifold
M
is
\beta
-smooth.

A natural way to define
\Phi_{y}
is in terms of the orthogonal projection
\pi_{y}:=\pi_{T_{y}M}
onto the tangent space
T_{y}M
at
y
. When
\tau>0
, the map
\pi_{y}
restricted to
M\cap B_{D}(y,\tau/4)
is one-to-one and
B_{T_{y}M}(0,\tau/8)\subset\pi_{y}(M\cap B_{D}(y,\tau/4))
, see for instance
[
1
]
. We can then define
\Phi_{y}
as the inverse
\pi_{y}\big|_{M\cap B_{D}(y,\tau/4)}
and
U_{y}=B_{T_{y}M}(0,\tau/8)
.

Similarly we say that a function
f:M\mapsto\mathbb{R}
is
\alpha
-smooth, if for any
y\in M
the function
f\circ\Phi_{y}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
is
\alpha
-smooth in the regular sense. The embedding of
M
into
\mathbb{R}^{D}
generates a natural Riemannian metric induced from
\mathbb{R}^{D}
together with a volume element
dy
known as the Hausdorff measure. Finally, we say that a measure
\mu
is
\alpha
-smooth if the measure
\mu\circ\Phi_{y}^{-1}
is
\alpha
-smooth on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, i.e. admits an
\alpha
-smooth density. If a measure
\mu
has a density
p
w.r.t.
dy
, on a chart
U_{y}
, by the change of variables formula, the corresponding density has the form
p(\Phi(z))\left|\nabla\Phi_{y}(z)\nabla^{T}\Phi_{y}(z)\right|^{-1}
, and since the Jacobian is only guaranteed to be
\beta-1
smooth, we will assume that
\alpha+1\leq\beta
.

To control the smoothness we recall the definition of the
C^{k}
norm for vector-valued functions. Let
f:\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\mapsto\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}
, then

\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{C^{k}(\Omega)}=\max_{0\leq i\leq k}\sup_{x\in\Omega}\left\lVert d^{i}f(x)\right\rVert_{op},

where
d^{i}f(x):\left(\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\right)^{\otimes i}\mapsto\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}
is the
i
th differential – a multilinear operator defined as

d^{i}f(x)\left(v_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes v_{n}\right)=d_{v_{1}}\ldots d_{v_{i}}f(x),

and
d_{v}f
is a directional derivative of
f
along the vector
v\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}
. Note that the
0
-th differential is the function itself, so
\sup_{x\in\Omega}\left\lVert d^{0}f(x)\right\rVert_{op}=\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\infty}
is the usual
\sup
-norm.

With all these definitions in mind, we are finally ready to present our assumptions on a manifold
M
.

{Assumption}

The support
M
of
\mu
is a compact
\beta
-smooth manifold embedded in
\mathbb{R}^{D}
of dimension
d\geq 1
and reach
\tau>\tau_{\min}
. Furthermore, there is a constant
L_{M}>0
s.t. for all
y\in M
the inverse projection function
\Phi_{y}
satisfies
\left\lVert\Phi_{y}\right\rVert_{C^{\beta}\left(B_{T_{y}M}(0,\tau/8)\right)}\leq L_{M}
.

Similarly, we define a corresponding class of measures.

{Assumption}

The measure
\mu
has an
\alpha
-smooth density
p(y)
w.r.t. Hausdorff measure on
M
satisfying
p_{\min}\leq p\leq p_{\max}
and there is a constant
L_{\mu}
such that for all
y\in M

{\left\lVert p\circ\Phi_{y}\right\rVert_{C^{\alpha}\left(B_{T_{y}M}(0,\tau/4)\right)}\leq L_{\mu}.}

Besides the dimension
d
, the complexity of the manifold additionally depends on its volume
\Vol M
and its local smoothness which we captured in terms of the reach
\tau
and Hölder constant
L_{M}
. The complexity of the measure
\mu
primarily depends on its similarity to uniform measure, and thus to
p_{\min}
and
p_{\max}
. To take it into account we introduce the constant
C_{\log}
defined below.

{Assumption}

We assume that there is a constant
C_{\log}>\max\left(\log 2d,4\right)
such that the following bounds hold: (i)
e^{-dC_{\log}}<p(y)<e^{dC_{\log}}
; (ii)
\log\Vol M<e^{dC_{\log}}
, (iii)
\min(\tau,L_{M}^{-1})\geq e^{-C_{\log}}
.

For convenience we introduce
r_{0}:=\min\left(1,\tau,L_{M}^{-1}\right)/8>0
– the radius of a neighborhood in which the functions
\Phi_{y}
for all
y\in M
are well behaved, see Appendix A for details.

Remark
.

Assumption
2.2
imposes different bounds on
r_{0}
and
\Vol
. Essentially, this follows from a relation between the radius and volume of the
d
-dimensional sphere, i.e.
{\Vol B_{d}(0,r)\propto r^{d}}
.

Note that in
[
48
]
and
[
10
]
the authors do not assume a lower bound on the density but they do not treat the case of degenerate distributions (here living on a submanifold of smaller dimension). In our proofs this assumption is used crucially in the step associated to learning the manifold (i.e. construction of
M_{i}^{*}
). We do not know if this is a necessary assumption but to our knowledge it appears in all papers controlling the error on estimating a distribution living on an unknown manifold.

The smoothness of the manifold is defined locally, therefore a common strategy is to find a dense set
\mathcal{G}
and then for each point,
G\in\mathcal{G}
consider a manifold in the
r_{0}
-vicinity of this point.
The following proposition bounds the size of such a set.

Proposition 2.1
.

Let
M\subset\mathbb{R}^{D}
be a
d
-dimensional manifold. For any
\varepsilon\in(0,r_{0})
, there exists a finite subset
\mathcal{G}=\{G_{1},\ldots,G_{N}\}\subset M
satisfying the following two properties:

1.

\varepsilon
-dense: For every
x\in M
, there exists
G_{i}\in\mathcal{G}
such that
\|x-G_{i}\|\leq\varepsilon
.

2.

(\varepsilon/2)
-sparse: For any distinct
G_{i},G_{j}\in\mathcal{G}
, it holds that
\|G_{i}-G_{j}\|\geq\varepsilon/2
.

Furthermore, the cardinality
N=N(\varepsilon)
of the set
\mathcal{G}
is bounded by:

N\leq(\varepsilon/2)^{-d}\operatorname{Vol}(M)

Proof.

Take
\mathcal{G}
as a maximal
\varepsilon
-separated set. By construction, the balls
M\cap B(G_{i},\varepsilon/2)
do not intersect, and
{\Vol\left(M\cap B(y,\varepsilon/2)\right)\geq(\varepsilon/2)^{d}}
for any
y\in M
, so
N(\varepsilon/2)^{d}\lesssim\Vol M
.
∎

Let
\varepsilon
and
G_{1},\ldots,G_{N}
be as in Proposition
2.1
.
Then we can build a partition
\left(G_{i},M_{i},p_{i}\right)
of a measure subordinated to the points
G_{1},\ldots,G_{N}
as follows. We take
M_{i}=\Phi_{i}\left(B_{d}(0,\varepsilon)\right)
, and define
p_{i}(y)=p(y)\phi_{i}(y)
, where
\phi_{1},\ldots,\phi_{N}
-is a smooth partition of unity subordinated to
M_{i}
and satisfying
\phi_{i}\big|_{B_{D}(G_{i},\varepsilon/2)}\equiv 1
.

2.3
Notation

Convention 1

To simplify notation we assume that
\diam M\leq 1
and
0\in M
. The general case can be obtained by a simple rescaling and shift.

Notation 1

We use
\lesssim,\gtrsim,\simeq
in cases when the corresponding inequality(equation) holds up to a multiplicative constant that depends only on
\beta,\alpha
, the quantities that are responsible for manifold and density regularity. We keep track of quantities
C_{\log},D,d
. Similarly, when we say that
n
is large enough we mean that
n\geq n_{0}
where
n_{0}
may depend on
C_{\log},d,\alpha,\beta
but not on
D
.

Notation 2

Throughout the paper we use
t,T
to denote time,
\varepsilon,\delta,\eta,\gamma<1
to denote errors;
D
,
d
denote the ambient and manifold dimensions respectively. Unless otherwise stated we assume that
\log t,\log T,\log\varepsilon,\log\delta,\log\eta,\log\gamma
, and
\log D
are all of order
\log n
. We also use the notation
\log_{+}x=\max\left(\log x,0\right)
.

Notation 3

Generally, we use the letters
x,y,z
to denote points in
\mathbb{R}^{D},M
, and a tangent space respectively.

Notation 4

We use capital
T_{k}
to denote the intervals on which we approximate the score function, while we use regular
t_{k}
to denote the discretization timesteps.

Following
[
31
]
we give the formal definition of the class of neural networks we will be working with.

Definition 1
.

The class
\Psi(L,W,S,B)
of
\relu
-neural networks with depth
L
, shape
W=\left(W_{1},\ldots,W_{L}\right)
, sparsity
S
, and norm constant
B

is

\Psi\left(L,W,S,B\right)=\Big\{\phi(x)=\left(A^{L}\relu+b^{L}\right)\circ\ldots\circ\left(A^{1}\relu+b^{1}\right)(x)\,\big|A^{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{W_{i}\times W_{i+1}},\\
b^{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{W_{i+1}},\sum_{i=1}^{L}(\left\lVert A^{i}\right\rVert_{0}+\left\lVert b^{i}\right\rVert_{0})\leq S,\sup_{i}(\left\lVert A^{i}\right\rVert_{\infty}+\left\lVert b^{i}\right\rVert_{\infty})\leq B\Big\}.

3
Approximation of the Score Function in High Dimension

Throughout this section let
\mu
be a measure on a
d
-dimensional manifold
M
satisfying Assumptions
2.2
–
2.2
, and
\mathcal{Y}=\{y_{1},\ldots,y_{n}\}
be i.i.d. samples from
\mu
.
Let
X_{t}
be the forward process (
2.1
) with initial condition
X_{0}\sim\mu
, denote as
p(t,x)
the density function (
2.3
) of
X_{t}
, and as
s(t,x)=\nabla\log p(t,x)
the score function (
2.5
).

Our first contribution is the construction of an estimator
\hat{s}
that converges to
s(t,x)
independently of the ambient dimension
D
with an almost optimal rate.
We prove this under the following technical assumption, which we believe can be relaxed with careful handling.

{Assumption}

If
d\geq 3
, the manifold
M
is
\beta
-smooth, where
\beta\geq\frac{d}{d-2}(\alpha+1)
, where
\alpha
is smoothness of measure
\mu
.

Theorem 3.1
.

Let
\mu
be an
\alpha
-smooth measure satisfying Assumptions
2.2
–
3
supported on a
d
-dimensional
\beta
-smooth manifold
M
embedded into
\mathbb{R}^{D}
.

Let
X_{t}
be the forward process (
2.1
) with initial condition
X_{0}\sim\mu
,
p(t,x)
be the density function (
2.3
) of
X_{t}
, and
s(t,x)=\nabla\log p(t,x)
be the score function (
2.5
).

Denote by
\mathcal{Y}=\{y_{1},\ldots,y_{n}\}
a set of
n
i.i.d. samples from
\mu
. Fix any positive
\gamma>1-1/d
. Let
\underline{T}\asymp(\log n)^{\gamma}n^{-\frac{2(\alpha+1)}{2\alpha+d}}
and
\overline{T}=O(\log n)
. Then, there exists an estimator
\hat{s}(t,x)
based on
\mathcal{Y}
and satisfying

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{Y}\sim\mu^{\otimes n}}\int_{\underline{T}}^{\overline{T}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{D}}\sigma^{2}_{t}\left\lVert\hat{s}(t,x)-s(t,x)\right\rVert^{2}p(t,x)dxdt\leq\begin{cases}n^{-\frac{2(\alpha+1)}{2\alpha+d}}\polylog n,&\text{ if }d\geq 3,\\
(\log n)^{\gamma}\cdot n^{-1}&\text{ otherwise }\end{cases}

(3.1)

for all
n\geq C_{0}
where
C_{0}
is independent of
D
but may depend on
\alpha,\beta,d,
and
C_{\log}
.

Moreover, for every fixed
A>0
there is a constant
C_{1}
depending on
\alpha,\beta,d,C_{\log}
and
A
, such that for all
n\geq C_{1}
, with probability at least
1-O\left(\polylog n\cdot n^{-\frac{\alpha+1}{2\alpha+d}}\right)
over the training sample
\mathcal{Y}
, the constructed estimator satisfies, for every
t\in[\underline{T},\overline{T}]
,

\displaystyle\rP_{X_{0},Z_{D}}\left(\left\lVert\sigma_{t}\hat{s}(t,X_{t})+Z_{D}\right\rVert>244\log n\,\middle|\,\mathcal{Y}\right)

\displaystyle\leq n^{-A}.

(3.2)

This theorem is a corollary of Theorem
3.3
presented in Section
3.1
, where we also present a detailed description of the estimator
\hat{s}(t,x)
.

Similar results were obtained in
[
39
,
31
]
, and we generally follow their strategy and build
\hat{s}
as a
\relu
-neural network (see Definition
1
). Our main improvement compared to the literature is that we achieve bounds that do not depend on the ambient dimension
D
, while in
[
39
]
this bound is multiplied by
D^{d/2+\alpha}
.

The key innovation leading to the result are new regularity bounds on
s(t,x)
presented in Section
4
, and the construction of an efficient manifold approximation algorithm in Section
5
.

Finally, in Section
6
, we show how to get rid of the constant
\sqrt{D}
in the Wasserstein convergence rate bound (
2.8
). To achieve this we leverage (
3.2
) and enforce consistency of the score function estimates along the trajectory.

Corollary 3.2
.

Let
\hat{s}
be the estimator constructed in Theorem
3.1
. If
\overline{T}=\log D+\log n=O(\log n)
, then Theorem
6.1
implies that there is a discretization scheme consisting of
O(n^{\frac{2\alpha+d}{\alpha+1}}\polylog n)
discretization steps achieving

\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{Y}\sim\mu^{\otimes n}}W_{1}(\mu,\hat{\mu})\lesssim\polylog n\cdot\begin{cases}n^{-\frac{(\alpha+1)}{2\alpha+d}},&\text{ if }d\geq 3,\\
n^{-1/2}&\text{ otherwise }\end{cases}

Proof.

The result is obtained by applying Theorem
6.1
to the estimator from Theorem
3.1
. See details in Appendix
E
.
∎

3.1
Construction of the estimator

In this subsection we describe the estimator used in
Theorem
3.1
. There are two cases. When

d\leq 2
, the empirical measure already gives the required score-matching
accuracy. When
d\geq 3
, we use the localized, low-dimensional construction
described below.

3.1.1
Construction when
d\leq 2

In the case
d\leq 2
, the construction is simple, as we can use the empirical
score to get the desired rate. Let

\hat{\mu}_{n}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\delta_{y_{j}}

be the empirical measure. We define
\hat{s}
as the score function associated to
the Gaussian convolution of
\hat{\mu}_{n}
:

\hat{s}(t,x)=\frac{1}{\sigma_{t}^{2}}\frac{\sum_{y_{j}\in\mathcal{Y}}e^{-\left\lVert x-c_{t}y_{j}\right\rVert^{2}/(2\sigma_{t}^{2})}\left(c_{t}y_{j}-x\right)}{\sum_{y_{j}\in\mathcal{Y}}e^{-\left\lVert x-c_{t}y_{j}\right\rVert^{2}/(2\sigma_{t}^{2})}}.

(3.3)

Proposition
5.6
bounds the score-matching error by

W_{2}(\mu,\hat{\mu}_{n})
, and the empirical Wasserstein bound
of
[
15
]
gives the
d\leq 2
rate in
Theorem
3.1
. The tail bound (
3.2
) for

\hat{s}
defined above is proved in
Appendix D.

3.1.2
Construction when
d\geq 3

For
d\geq 3
, the construction is blockwise in time. We split

[\underline{T},\overline{T}]=\bigcup_{k=0}^{K-1}[T_{k},T_{k+1}],\qquad T_{k+1}/T_{k}=2,

and we construct an estimator
\hat{s}_{k}
on each block, and then set

\hat{s}(t,\cdot)=\hat{s}_{k}(t,\cdot)
for

t\in[T_{k},T_{k+1}]
. Below, we fix a block and suppress the index
k

when this causes no ambiguity. We use Tweedie’s formula (
2.14
) and write the
estimator in the form

\hat{s}_{k}(t,x)=\frac{c_{t}\hat{e}_{k}(t,x)-x}{\sigma_{t}^{2}},\qquad t\in[T_{k},T_{k+1}],

where
\hat{e}_{k}
is an estimator of

\mathbb{E}[X_{0}\mid X_{t}=x]
,
1
1

1

where
(X_{0},X_{t})\sim\mu(dx_{0})p_{t}(dx_{t}|x_{0})

whose construction is detailed below.
Let
k\leq K-1
,
define the integer
N=N(T_{k},n)
, up to integer rounding, by

\displaystyle N(T_{k},n):=\begin{cases}\max\left((\log n)^{2\gamma d}(\sigma_{T_{k}}/c_{T_{k}})^{-d},n\cdot n^{-\frac{2(\alpha+1)}{2\alpha+d}}\right),&T_{k}\geq n^{-\frac{2}{2\alpha+d}},\\
(\log n)^{-\gamma d}n^{\frac{d}{2\alpha+d}},&T_{k}<n^{-\frac{2}{2\alpha+d}}.\end{cases}

Take
G_{1},\ldots,G_{N}
from the training sample
\mathcal{Y}
, set

\varepsilon_{N}\asymp\left(\frac{\log N}{N}\right)^{1/d},

and define

\mathcal{V}_{i}=\{G_{j}-G_{i}:\left\lVert G_{j}-G_{i}\right\rVert\leq\varepsilon_{N}\},\qquad\mathcal{H}_{i}=\spn\mathcal{V}_{i},\qquad d_{i}=\dim\mathcal{H}_{i}.

Let
P_{\mathcal{H}_{i}}:\mathbb{R}^{d_{i}}\to\mathcal{H}_{i}\subset\mathbb{R}^{D}
be a linear isometry
identifying
\mathcal{H}_{i}
with
\mathbb{R}^{d_{i}}
. These objects are fixed once the
training sample is fixed and are not optimized over in the empirical risk
minimization.

For
x\in\mathbb{R}^{D}
, define

i_{\min}(x):=\argmin_{i\leq N}\left\lVert x-c_{t}G_{i}\right\rVert,\qquad G_{\min}(x):=G_{i_{\min}(x)}.

Let
\rho:\mathbb{R}_{+}\to[0,1]
be

\displaystyle\rho(u)=\begin{cases}1,&0\leq u\leq 1/2,\\
2-2u,&1/2<u<1,\\
0,&u\geq 1.\end{cases}

We define

\displaystyle\rho_{i}(t,x)=\rho\left(\frac{\left\lVert x-c_{t}G_{i}\right\rVert^{2}-\left\lVert x-c_{t}G_{\min}(x)\right\rVert^{2}}{2C(T_{k},n)}\right),\quad C(T_{k},n)=\begin{cases}\sigma_{T_{k}}^{2}\log n\log\log n,&T_{k}\geq n^{-\frac{2}{2\alpha+d}},\\
6\varepsilon_{N}^{2},&T_{k}<n^{-\frac{2}{2\alpha+d}},\end{cases}

and the local multipliers

\displaystyle h_{i}(t,x)=\begin{cases}\exp\left(\frac{\left\lVert x-c_{t}G_{\min}(x)\right\rVert^{2}-\left\lVert x-c_{t}G_{i}\right\rVert^{2}}{2\sigma_{t}^{2}}\right),&t\geq n^{-\frac{2}{2\alpha+d}},\\
1,&t<n^{-\frac{2}{2\alpha+d}}.\end{cases}

Note that by construction
h_{i}(t,x)\leq 1
. Write
\widetilde{\rho}_{i}(t,x):=h_{i}(t,x)\rho_{i}(t,x)
and

\widetilde{x}_{i,t}:=P_{\mathcal{H}_{i}}^{T}(x-c_{t}G_{i})\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{i}}.

The estimator
\hat{s}_{k}
is obtained by minimizing

\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{Y}}(\phi,T_{k},T_{k+1})
over neural networks with the architecture

\phi(t,x)=\frac{c_{t}}{\sigma_{t}^{2}}\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{N}\widetilde{\rho}_{i}(t,x)\phi_{w_{i}}(t,\widetilde{x}_{i,t})\left(G_{i}+P_{\mathcal{H}_{i}}\phi_{e_{i}}(t,\widetilde{x}_{i,t})\right)}{\sum_{i=1}^{N}\widetilde{\rho}_{i}(t,x)\phi_{w_{i}}(t,\widetilde{x}_{i,t})}-\frac{x}{\sigma_{t}^{2}}.

(3.4)

Here

\phi_{e_{i}}:\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{i}}\to\mathbb{R}^{d_{i}},\qquad\phi_{w_{i}}:\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{i}}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}

are neural networks. More formally, we consider the class

\mathcal{S}_{k}=\Big\{\phi\text{ of the form~\eqref{eq:definition of phi}}:\phi_{e_{i}},\phi_{w_{i}}\in\Psi(L,W,S,B),\quad\left\lVert\phi_{e_{i}}\right\rVert\leq C_{2},\\
\phi_{w_{i}}(t,\widetilde{x}_{i,t})\geq n^{-2},\quad\left\|\frac{c_{t}\phi_{e_{i}}(t,\widetilde{x}_{i,t})-\widetilde{x}_{i,t}}{\sigma_{t}}\right\|_{\infty}\leq\log n\Big\},

(3.5)

where the last two constraints are imposed on the relevant local-network
domains and
C_{2}\geq 2\diam M
. The network parameters satisfy

L=O(\polylog n),\qquad\left\lVert W\right\rVert_{\infty}=O(\polylog n),\qquad S=O(\polylog n),\qquad B=e^{O(\polylog n)}.

The important point is that
P_{\mathcal{H}_{i}}
,
G_{i}
, and the cutoffs are
explicit functions of the training sample, and are not optimized in the
empirical risk minimization.

For comparison, one may also represent the construction by a single
ambient neural network and optimize over the coordinate maps implicitly.
This gives the larger class

\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{k}=\{\phi^{\prime}\in\Psi(L^{\prime},W^{\prime},S^{\prime},B^{\prime}):\left\lVert\phi^{\prime}\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq C^{\prime}_{2}\frac{\sqrt{D}\log n}{\sigma_{t}}\},

(3.6)

where

L^{\prime}=O(\polylog n),\qquad S^{\prime}=O(N\polylog n),\qquad B^{\prime}=e^{O(\polylog n)},

and
W^{\prime}_{i}=O(N\polylog n)
for hidden layers while the input and output
layers have ambient dimension
D
.

The following blockwise theorem is the version used to prove
Theorem
3.1
.

Theorem 3.3
.

Let
d\geq 3
, and consider the assumptions of
Theorem
3.1
. Let
k\leq K-1

and

\hat{s}_{k}\in\argmin_{\phi\in\mathcal{S}_{k}}\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{Y}}(\phi,T_{k},T_{k+1})

be an empirical risk minimizer over the class
\mathcal{S}_{k}
defined
in (
3.5
), with

L,\left\lVert W\right\rVert_{\infty},S=O(\polylog n),\qquad B=e^{O(\polylog n)}.

Then, for all
n
large enough,
with constants independent of
D
,

\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{Y}\sim\mu^{\otimes n}}\int_{T_{k}}^{T_{k+1}}\mathbb{E}\left\lVert\hat{s}_{k}(t,X_{t})-s(t,X_{t})\right\rVert^{2}\,dt\lesssim\polylog n\min\left(\sigma_{T_{k}}^{-2}n^{-\frac{2(\alpha+1)}{2\alpha+d}},n^{-\frac{2\alpha}{2\alpha+d}}\right).

If
\hat{s}^{\prime}_{k}
is the empirical risk minimizer over the ambient class

\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{k}
in (
3.6
),

\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{Y}\sim\mu^{\otimes n}}\int_{T_{k}}^{T_{k+1}}\mathbb{E}\left\lVert\hat{s}^{\prime}_{k}(t,X_{t})-s(t,X_{t})\right\rVert^{2}\,dt\lesssim D\polylog n\min\left(\sigma_{T_{k}}^{-2}n^{-\frac{2(\alpha+1)}{2\alpha+d}},n^{-\frac{2\alpha}{2\alpha+d}}\right).

Assembling
\hat{s}(t,\cdot)=\hat{s}_{k}(t,\cdot)
for

t\in[T_{k},T_{k+1}]
gives the bound (
3.1
)
corresponding to the case
d\geq 3
of
Theorem
3.1
. The preliminary construction of

\mathcal{H}_{i}
is the dimension-reduction step: it reduces the approximation
problem from
\mathbb{R}^{D}
to
N
local problems of dimension

d_{i}=O(\log n)
.
Note that in Theorem
3.3
, there is a phase transition corresponding to

\sigma_{T_{k}}^{-2}n^{-\frac{2(\alpha+1)}{2\alpha+d}}=n^{-\frac{2\alpha}{2\alpha+d}}\quad\Leftrightarrow T_{k}=n^{-2/(2\alpha+d)}.

As will be apparent in the sketch of the proof below, this phase transition also corresponds to
\sigma_{T_{k}}=\epsilon_{N}=n^{-1/(2\alpha+d)}
, which is also the optimal bandwidth in Kernel estimation.

3.1.3
Proof sketch for
d\geq 3

The proof starts from a bias-variance decomposition of the generalisation bound given in Theorem D.7, in Appendix D.5 which has the form

\displaystyle\int_{T_{k}}^{T_{k+1}}\mathbb{E}\left\lVert\hat{s}_{k}(t,X_{t})-s(t,X_{t})\right\rVert^{2}\,dt

\displaystyle\lesssim\inf_{\phi\in\mathcal{S}_{k}}\int_{T_{k}}^{T_{k+1}}\mathbb{E}\left\lVert\phi(t,X_{t})-s(t,X_{t})\right\rVert^{2}\,dt

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{(\log n)^{6}}{n}\log\mathcal{N}(\delta_{n})+\delta_{n},

where the expectation is with respect to both
X_{t}
and
\mathcal{Y}
and where
\log\mathcal{N}(\delta_{n})
is an entropy term.
The
substantive part of the proof is then the control on the bias term: we must exhibit an element of
\mathcal{S}_{k}

that approximates
s
.

By Tweedie’s formula, this is a problem about approximating the posterior
mean

s(t,x)=\frac{c_{t}e(t,x)-x}{\sigma_{t}^{2}},\qquad e(t,x)=\frac{\int_{M}y\,e^{-\left\lVert x-c_{t}y\right\rVert^{2}/(2\sigma_{t}^{2})}\mu(dy)}{\int_{M}e^{-\left\lVert x-c_{t}y\right\rVert^{2}/(2\sigma_{t}^{2})}\mu(dy)}.

First we approximate
M
by piecewise polynomial patches. To do so, we use the polynomial approximation
\Phi_{i}^{*}
of the true local parametrization
\Phi_{i}:B_{d}(0,r_{0})\rightarrow M
of
M
around
G_{i}
proposed in
[
2
]
.
We prove in Section
5
, that on an event of probability greater than
1-Cn^{-(\alpha+1)/(2\alpha+d)}\polylog n

\left\lVert\Phi_{i}^{*}-\Phi_{i}\right\rVert_{\infty}\lesssim\varepsilon_{N}^{\beta},\quad\text{and }\quad\Phi_{i}^{*}(B_{d}(0,8\varepsilon_{N}))\subset G_{i}+\mathcal{H}_{i},\quad d_{i}=\text{dim}(\mathcal{H}_{i})\lesssim\log n,

and the sets
\{G_{i},i\leq N\}
are
\varepsilon_{N}
-dense in
M
.
Thus the unknown embedded manifold is replaced by a union of polynomial
pieces living in spaces
\mathcal{H}_{i}
of dimension
d_{i}\lesssim\log n
.

The next important step is the localization of the posterior distribution of
X_{0}
given
X_{t}
. Theorem
4.4
in Section
4
implies that with high probability the posterior mass of
X_{0}
given
X_{t}
is concentrated on

B_{M}(X_{0},r_{t}):=M\cap B_{D}(X_{0},r_{t}),\quad\text{with}\quad r_{t}\asymp\frac{\sigma_{t}}{c_{t}}\sqrt{d\left(\log_{+}(c_{t}/\sigma_{t})+C_{\log}\right)+\log n}.

This implies that the integrals over
M
in the definition of
e(t,x)
can be restricted to
B_{M}(X_{0},r_{t})=\cup_{i=1}^{N}B_{M}(X_{0},r_{t})\cap\phi_{i}(B_{d}(0,8\varepsilon_{N}))
. The set of indices where the intersection is non empty depends on the unobserved
X_{0}
.
The cutoff functions
\rho_{i}
are an observable approximation of
1_{\text{dis}(X_{0},\Phi_{i}(B_{d}(0,8\varepsilon_{N})))\leq r_{t}}
. Indeed, the distance comparison in
Lemma
4.5
, combined with the localization event, gives
the following high-probability cutoff property:

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{ll}\text{when }\,T_{k}\geq n^{-2/(2\alpha+d)}:&\dist(X_{0},M_{i})<r_{t}\ \Longrightarrow\ \rho_{i}(t,X_{t})=1,\\[3.99994pt]
&\rho_{i}(t,X_{t})>0\ \Longrightarrow\ \dist(X_{0},M_{i})\lesssim r_{t}\sqrt{\log\log n},\\[8.00003pt]
\text{when }\,T_{k}<n^{-2/(2\alpha+d)}:&\rho_{i}(t,X_{t})>0\ \Longrightarrow\ B_{M}(X_{0},r_{t})\subset M_{i}.\end{array}

The two regimes in
T_{k}

then use the same architecture (
3.4
) in different ways.

Large times:
T_{k}\geq n^{-2/(2\alpha+d)}
. In this regime,
\varepsilon_{N}=o(r_{t})

so that there are multiple indices
i
for which
\rho_{i}\neq 0
. We then define
M_{i}=\Phi_{i}(B_{d}(0,8\varepsilon_{N}))
,
M_{i}^{*}=\Phi_{i}^{*}(B_{d}(0,8\varepsilon_{N}))\subset G_{i}+\mathcal{H}_{i}
, a decomposition of
\mu=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mu_{i}
based on a partition of unity with supports
M_{i}
and
\mu^{*}=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mu_{i}^{*}
constructed from the push forward measures
\mu_{i}^{*}
of
\mu_{i}
by
\Phi_{i}^{*}\circ\Phi_{i}^{-1}
. Then Proposition
5.6
in Section
5
shows that with high probability

\|e(t,X_{t})-e_{tr}^{*}(t,X_{t})\|^{2}\lesssim\sigma_{t}^{2}W_{2}^{2}(\mu,\mu^{*})\lesssim\sigma_{t}^{2}\varepsilon_{N}^{2\beta},\quad e_{tr}^{*}=\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{N}\rho_{i}(t,X_{t})p_{i}^{*}(t,X_{t})e_{i}^{*}(t,X_{t})}{\sum_{i=1}^{N}\rho_{i}(t,X_{t})p_{i}^{*}(t,X_{t})},

where
e_{i}^{*}(t,X_{t})
is the posterior expectation of
X_{0}
given
X_{t}
under the prior
\mu_{i}^{*}
and

p_{i}^{*}(t,X_{t})=\int_{M_{i}^{*}}e^{-\frac{\|X_{t}-c_{t}y\|^{2}}{2\sigma_{t}^{2}}}\mu_{i}^{*}(dy)=e^{-\frac{\|X_{t}-c_{t}X_{i,t}\|^{2}}{2\sigma_{t}^{2}}}\int_{M_{i}^{*}}e^{-\frac{(\|X_{t}-c_{t}y\|^{2}-\|X_{t}-c_{t}X_{i,t}\|^{2})}{2\sigma_{t}^{2}}}\mu_{i}^{*}(dy),

and
X_{i,t}=c_{t}G_{i}+pr_{\mathcal{H}_{i}}(X_{t}-c_{t}G_{i})
is the orthogonal projection of
X_{t}
onto
c_{t}G_{i}+\mathcal{H}_{i}
. Noting that
\|X_{t}-X_{i,t}\|^{2}-\|X_{t}-c_{t}G_{i}\|^{2}=-\|X_{i,t}-c_{t}G_{i}\|^{2}
, we obtain that

p_{i}^{*}(t,X_{t})=e^{-\frac{\|X_{t}-c_{t}G_{i}\|^{2}}{2\sigma_{t}^{2}}}\int_{M_{i}^{*}}e^{-\frac{(\|X_{i,t}-c_{t}y\|^{2}-\|X_{i,t}-c_{t}G_{i}\|^{2})}{2\sigma_{t}^{2}}}\mu_{i}^{*}(dy).

Written in the coordinates of
G_{i}+\mathcal{H}_{i}
, this
gives the approximating denoiser

e_{\mathrm{large}}(t,x)=\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{N}\widetilde{\rho}_{i}(t,x)\widetilde{p}_{i}^{*}(t,\widetilde{x}_{i,t})\left(G_{i}+P_{\mathcal{H}_{i}}\widetilde{e}_{i}^{*}(t,\widetilde{x}_{i,t})\right)}{\sum_{i=1}^{N}\widetilde{\rho}_{i}(t,x)\widetilde{p}_{i}^{*}(t,\widetilde{x}_{i,t})},

where
\widetilde{p}_{i}^{*}
and
\widetilde{e}_{i}^{*}
are the local density and
conditional mean generated by the
i
-th polynomial piece, written in the
coordinates of
\mathcal{H}_{i}
. This has exactly the same structure
as (
3.4
):
\phi_{w_{i}}
approximates

\widetilde{p}_{i}^{*}
, while
\phi_{e_{i}}
approximates

\widetilde{e}_{i}^{*}
.

Small times:
T_{k}\leq n^{-2(2\alpha+d)}
.
Here
\varepsilon_{N}=o(r_{t})
. The cutoff property says that for every active
i
(such that
\rho_{i}>0
),
\Phi_{i}(B_{d}(0,M_{i})
contains
B_{M}(X_{0},r_{t})
and is too large. To better localize we construct, in Appendix D.2, a
better
local chart
M_{i}
with radius
r_{t}
such that the conditional mean of
X_{0}
given
X_{t}
under the restriction of
\mu
on
M_{i}
,
e_{i}(t,X_{t})
, is very close to
e(t,X_{t})
with high probability. This implies that

e(t,X_{t})\approx\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{N}\rho_{i}(t,X_{t})e_{i}(t,X_{t})}{\sum_{i=1}^{N}\rho_{i}(t,X_{t})}.

We then approximate
e_{i}
for each active
i
by expressing each integral in
e_{i}
in terms of the coordinates
z=\Phi_{i}^{-1}(y)
, and by Taylor expanding the density
p(z)
of
\mu
and approximating
y=\Phi_{i}(z)
by
\Phi_{i}^{*}(z)
.
This leads to low dimensional polynomial approximations of the numerator and denominator defining
e_{i}
which leads to the architecture (
3.4
).

Combining the manifold-approximation error, the localization error, and the
low-dimensional neural-network approximation gives an approximant in

\mathcal{S}_{k}
with the rate in Theorem
3.3
. A bound on the entropy term
\mathcal{N}(\delta_{n})

then yields the ERM bound.

4
High Probability Bounds on the Score Function

In this section we present our main tool, the high-probability bounds on the score function
s(t,X_{t})
under the manifold hypothesis w.r.t.
X_{t}
– the forward process (
2.1
). These results play a key role in the approximation of the score presented in Section
3.1
.

We start with the study of how
D
-dimensional Gaussian noise
Z_{D}\sim\mathcal{N}\left(0,\Id_{D}\right)
interacts with the smooth
d
-dimensional manifold
M
. The following theorem bounds the scalar product between
Z_{D}
and vectors connecting points on the manifold.

Proposition 4.1
.

Let
M
be a
\beta\geq 1
-smooth manifold satisfying Assumption
2.2
. Then for any
\delta>0
and
\varepsilon<r_{0}
with probability
1-\delta
for all
y,y^{\prime}\in M

\left|\left\langle Z_{D},y-y^{\prime}\right\rangle\right|\leq 4\varepsilon\sqrt{d}+\left(\left\lVert y-y^{\prime}\right\rVert+6\varepsilon\right)\sqrt{4d\log 2\varepsilon^{-1}+4\log_{+}\Vol M+2\log 2\delta^{-1}}.

(4.1)

Proof.

See Appendix B.1 for the complete proof.
Adapting classical results on the maximum of Gaussian processes we show that for any
L
-Lipschitz function
f:\mathbb{R}^{d}\mapsto\mathbb{R}^{D}
satisfying
f(0)=0

\rP\left(\sup_{z\in B_{d}(0,r)}\left\langle Z_{D},f(z)\right\rangle\leq Lr\left(\sqrt{d}+\sqrt{2\log\delta^{-1}}\right)\right)\geq 1-\delta.

Then, for a point
y\in M
we locally represent the manifold as
\Phi_{y}\left(B_{d}(0,\varepsilon)\right)
, see Section
2.2
, and applying the inequality above to
f(z)=\Phi_{y}(z)-\Phi_{y}(0)
we get a local version of inequality (
4.1
). Finally, taking an
\varepsilon
-dense set and applying the chaining argument we generalize the local result to the whole manifold
M
.
∎

Remark
.

Proposition
4.1
is an adaptation to the case of smooth regular manifolds of results related to the connection between metric entropy and Gaussian processes, see e.g.

[
44
, Chapter 5]
or
[
41
, Chapter 9]
. This connects our approach with
[
27
]
on sampling complexity of diffusion models.

The above theorem shows that the inner-product between the Gaussian vector
Z_{D}
and vectors on the manifold does not depend on the ambient dimension. In particular, using standard bounds on the length of Gaussian vectors, this means that the correlation between
Z_{D}
and
y-y^{\prime}
is
\lesssim\sqrt{d/D}
with high probability, implying that the noise added during the forward diffusion process is almost orthogonal to the manifold if
D\gg d
.

Assuming that
\beta\geq 2
and applying the same technique it is also possible to bound the projection of
Z_{D}
onto tangent spaces
T_{y}M
, uniformly in
y\in M
.

Proposition 4.2
.

Let
M
be a
\beta\geq 2
-smooth manifold satisfying Assumption
2.2
. Then for any
\delta>0
with probability at least
1-\delta
for all
y\in M

\left\lVert\pr_{T_{y}M}Z_{D}\right\rVert\leq 8\sqrt{d\left(4\log 2d+2\log r^{-1}_{0}+2\log_{+}\Vol M+2\log\delta^{-1}\right)}.

Equipped with the bounds on the scalar product between the noise
Z_{D}
and the vectors on the manifold
M
we are ready to analyze the score function. Let
\mu
be a measure satisfying Assumptions
2.2
–
2.2
. We recall Tweedie’s formula (
2.14
) stating that

s(t,x)=\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}_{t}}\frac{\int_{M}\left(c_{t}y-x\right)e^{-\left\lVert x-c_{t}y\right\rVert^{2}/2\sigma^{2}_{t}}p(y)dy}{\int_{M}e^{-\left\lVert x-c_{t}y\right\rVert^{2}/2\sigma^{2}_{t}}p(y)dy}=\int_{M}\frac{c_{t}y-x}{\sigma^{2}_{t}}p(y|t,x)dy,

where
p(y|t,x)\propto e^{-\left\lVert x-c_{t}y\right\rVert^{2}/2\sigma^{2}_{t}}p(y)
is normalized to be a density function corresponding to the random variable
\left(X_{0}\big|X_{t}=x\right)
.
In other words, the score function is the expectation of the function
\left(c_{t}y-x\right)/{\sigma^{2}_{t}}
w.r.t. the measure on
M
with density
p(y|t,x)
.
The density itself is a product of two components:
p(y)dy\propto dy
which is proportional to the uniform measure
M
up to
p_{\min},p_{\max}
and

e^{-\left\lVert x-c_{t}y\right\rVert^{2}/2\sigma^{2}_{t}}
which exponentially penalizes points far from
x
.

As we have shown in Proposition
4.1
the noise vector
Z_{D}
is almost orthogonal to vectors on
M
. Therefore, representing the forward process (
2.1
) as
X_{t}=c_{t}X_{0}+\sigma_{t}Z_{D}
we write

\left\lVert X_{t}-c_{t}y\right\rVert^{2}=c^{2}_{t}\left\lVert X_{0}-y\right\rVert^{2}+\sigma^{2}_{t}\left\lVert Z_{D}\right\rVert^{2}+2c_{t}\sigma_{t}\left\langle X_{0}-y,Z_{D}\right\rangle\approx c^{2}_{t}\left\lVert X_{0}-y\right\rVert^{2}+\sigma^{2}_{t}\left\lVert Z_{D}\right\rVert^{2}.

Since the vector
Z_{D}
does not depend on
y
, it disappears during the normalization and therefore, roughly speaking, for small
t<r^{2}_{0}
, the log density function
\log p(y|t,X_{t})\approx-c^{2}_{t}\left\lVert X_{0}-y\right\rVert^{2}/2\sigma_{t}^{2}
looks like log the density of
d
-dimensional Gaussian variable on
T_{X_{0}}M
. The next proposition formalizes this argument.

Proposition 4.3
.

Let measure
\mu
satisfy Assumptions
2.2
–
2.2
and be supported on
M
.
For any
\delta>0
with probability at least
1-\delta
on
X_{t}
the following bound holds uniformly in
y\in M

-20d\left(\log_{+}(c_{t}/\sigma_{t})+4C_{\log}\right)-8\log\delta^{-1}-\frac{3}{4}(c_{t}/\sigma_{t})^{2}\left\lVert X_{0}-y\right\rVert^{2}\\
\leq\log p(y|t,X_{t})\\
\leq 20d\left(\log_{+}(c_{t}/\sigma_{t})+4C_{\log}\right)+8\log\delta^{-1}-\frac{1}{4}(c_{t}/\sigma_{t})^{2}\left\lVert X_{0}-y\right\rVert^{2}.

(4.2)

Proposition
4.3
is proved in Appendix B.2.
So, the density
p(y|t,X_{t})
exponentially penalizes for being too far away from
X_{0}
, and, therefore, most of the mass of the measure with density
p(y|t,X_{t})
concentrates in a ball centered at
X_{0}
.

Theorem 4.4
.

Let measure
\mu
satisfy Assumptions
2.2
–
2.2
and be supported on
M
. Fix
t,\delta,\eta>0
and define

r(t,\delta,\eta)=2(\sigma_{t}/c_{t})\sqrt{20d\left(\log_{+}(c_{t}/\sigma_{t})+4C_{\log}\right)+8\log\delta^{-1}+\log\eta^{-1}}.

1.

A.s.in
X_{0}\sim\mu
and with probability
1-\delta
in
Z_{D}\sim\mathcal{N}\left(0,\Id_{D}\right)
for a random point
X_{t}=c_{t}X_{0}+\sigma_{t}Z_{D}
the following holds

\int_{y\in M:\left\lVert y-X_{0}\right\rVert\leq r(t,\delta,\eta)}p(y|t,X_{t})dy\geq 1-\eta.

(4.3)

2.

As a corollary, a.s. in
X_{0}\sim\mu
and with probability
1-\delta
in
Z_{D}\sim\mathcal{N}\left(0,\Id_{D}\right)
for a random point
X_{t}=c_{t}X_{0}+\sigma_{t}Z_{D}
the following holds

\left\lVert\sigma_{t}s(t,X_{t})+Z_{D}\right\rVert\leq 4\sqrt{20d\left(2\log_{+}(c_{t}/\sigma_{t})+4C_{\log}\right)+8\log\delta^{-1}}.

3.

Integrating over
Z_{D}
, for all
y\in M

\mathbb{E}_{Z_{D}}\left\lVert\sigma_{t}s(t,c_{t}y+\sigma_{t}Z_{D})+Z_{D}\right\rVert^{2}\leq 8\left(40d\log_{+}(c_{t}/\sigma_{t})+80dC_{\log}+3\right).

(4.4)

The proof of the above proposition is given in Appendix B.2.

In particular, the result shows that on average the score function computed at point
X_{t}=c_{t}X_{0}+\sigma_{t}Z_{D}
estimates the added noise
Z_{D}/\sigma_{t}
with an error scaling as
\sqrt{d}/\sigma_{t}
up to
\log
-factor, and thereby the estimate
c^{-1}_{t}\left(X_{t}+\sigma^{2}_{t}s(t,X_{t})\right)

of point
X_{0}
has an error
(\sigma_{t}/c_{t})\sqrt{d}
up to
\log
factor independent on
D
.

Finally, let us present the result, that will play an important role in the construction of weight functions
\rho_{i}(t,x)
discussed in Section
3
.

Lemma 4.5
.

Let
\mathcal{G}=\{G_{1},\ldots,G_{N}\}
be an
\varepsilon
-dense set on
M
. Denote the nearest neighbor of
X_{t}
in
\mathcal{G}
as
G_{\min}(t)=G_{\min}(X_{t}):=\arg\min_{i}\left\lVert X_{t}-c_{t}G_{i}\right\rVert
. With probability at least
1-\delta
for all
G_{i}

\frac{2}{3}c^{-2}_{t}\left(\left\lVert X_{t}-c_{t}G_{i}\right\rVert^{2}-\left\lVert X_{t}-c_{t}G_{\min}(t)\right\rVert^{2}\right)-128(\sigma_{t}/c_{t})^{2}\left(d\log_{+}(c_{t}/\sigma_{t})+4dC_{\log}+\log\delta^{-1}\right)\\
\leq\left\lVert X_{0}-G_{i}\right\rVert^{2}\leq\\
9\varepsilon^{2}+2c^{-2}_{t}\left(\left\lVert X_{t}-c_{t}G_{i}\right\rVert^{2}-\left\lVert X_{t}-c_{t}G_{\min}(t)\right\rVert^{2}\right)+128(\sigma_{t}/c_{t})^{2}\left(d\log_{+}(c_{t}/\sigma_{t})+4dC_{\log}+\log\delta^{-1}\right).

(4.5)

This lemma is proved in Appendix B.3.

5
Manifold Approximation

In this section, we discuss how to efficiently approximate the manifold
M:=\supp\mu
, using a piecewise polynomial surface
M^{*}
, an important step in the construction of estimator
\hat{s}(t,x)
to the true score
s(t,x)
.

Definition 2
.

A piecewise polynomial approximation of
M
of order
\beta
is determined through an integer
N
, a positive scale parameter
\varepsilon_{N}<r_{0}
, an
\varepsilon_{N}
dense set
\mathcal{G}=\{G_{1},\ldots,G_{N}\}
on
M
, and a set of functions
\{\Phi_{1}^{*},\ldots,\Phi_{N}^{*}\}
, where
\Phi_{i}^{*}:B_{d}(0,\varepsilon_{N})\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{D}
are polynomials of order
\beta-1
satisfying
\Phi^{*}_{i}(0)=G_{i}
.
The approximating surface
M^{*}
is then defined as
M^{*}=\bigcup\Phi^{*}_{i}\left(B_{d}(0,\varepsilon_{N})\right),
and each
\Phi^{*}_{i}
plays the role of a polynomial approximation of the local inverse of the projection on the tangent space
T_{G_{i}}M
.

Finally, we call an approximation optimal if it satisfies the following two conditions: (i)
\log N\lesssim d\log\varepsilon^{-1}_{N}
; (ii) for each
i\leq N,k\leq\beta
and any
z\in B_{d}(0,\varepsilon_{N})
it holds that
\left\lVert\nabla^{k}\Phi_{i}(z)-\nabla^{k}\Phi^{*}_{i}(z)\right\rVert_{op}\leq L^{*}\varepsilon_{N}^{\beta-k}
for some constant
L^{*}>0
.

Example 1
.

The natural way to construct an optimal approximation is to fix
\varepsilon_{N}
, take as
\mathcal{G}
a minimal
\varepsilon_{N}
-dense set
\mathcal{G}=\{G_{1},\ldots,G_{N}\}
, and choose
N=\left|\mathcal{G}\right|
. We recall that by Proposition
2.1
,
N\leq(\varepsilon_{N}/2)^{-d}\Vol M
, so condition (i) is satisfied. Then, let
\Phi^{*}_{i}
be the Taylor approximation of
\Phi_{G_{i}}
of order
\beta-1
; by Assumption
2.2
we have the uniform bound
\left\lVert\Phi_{G_{i}}\right\rVert_{C^{\beta}\left(B_{d}(0,\varepsilon_{N})\right)}\leq L_{M}
for all
i\leq N
, so assumption (ii) is also satisfied.

We use Example
1
to construct the neural network in (ii) of Theorem
3.3
which leads to a suboptimal dependence in
D
. To improve upon it we use a different approximation based on
[
2
]
which leads to the bound in (i) of Theorem
3.3
.

Having
M^{*}
, we can approximate
\mu
with a measure
\mu^{*}
supported on
M^{*}
, by first representing
\mu=\sum\mu_{i},
where
\supp\mu_{i}\subset M_{i}
and then taking
\mu^{*}=\sum\mu^{*}_{i}
, where
\mu^{*}_{i}=\Phi^{*}_{i}\circ\Phi^{-1}_{i}\mu_{i}
– is the pushforward of
\mu_{i}
from
M_{i}
to
M^{*}_{i}
. Finally, we define
s^{*}(t,x)
as the score function corresponding to the forward process (
2.1
) with initial distribution
\mu^{*}
.

In the following section we construct the local polynomial approximations
\Phi_{i}^{*}
of
\Phi_{i}
which are keys in the construction of the neural network approximating
s(t,x)
, as explained in Section
3.1
.

5.1
Support Estimation

We start the discussion by answering the first question.
The approach described in Example
1
has a major flaw: when
M
is unknown the dimension of the search space consisting of unknown polynomials
\Phi^{*}_{G_{i}}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{D}
scales with
D
and the number of parameters of the resulting estimator blows up.

However, we have access to samples
\mathcal{Y}=\{y_{1},\ldots,y_{n}\}
which already contain some information about
M
, and as has been shown in
[
2
]
one can build an estimator of
M
using
\mathcal{Y}
with convergence rate independent of the extrinsic dimension
D
. We modify this argument to make the size of the search space also independent of
D
.

As a first step, we fix an arbitrary
N\leq n
large enough and take as
\mathcal{G}
the subset
\mathcal{G}=\{G_{i}:=y_{i},i\leq N\}
. Note that
\mathcal{G}
is a set of
N
i.i.d. samples from the measure
\mu
.
[
3
]
has shown that with high probability for an appropriate choice of
\varepsilon_{N}
the set
\mathcal{G}
is
\varepsilon_{N}
-dense.

Proposition 5.1
.

[
3
, Lemma III.23]

Let the measure
\mu
satisfy Assumption
2.2
and
\mathcal{G}=\{G_{1},\ldots,G_{N}\}
be
N
i.i.d. samples from
\mu
. For any
\varepsilon_{N}\geq\left(\frac{\beta C_{d}}{p_{\min}}\frac{\log N}{N}\right)^{1/d}
where
C_{d}>0
is large enough and depends only on
d
, for
N
large enough so that
\varepsilon_{N}<r_{0}
, with probability
1-N^{-\frac{\beta}{d}}
, the set
\mathcal{G}
is
\varepsilon_{N}/2
-dense on
M
.

Applying Proposition
5.1
we represent the manifold as
M=\bigcup_{i=1}^{N}\Phi_{G_{i}}\left(B_{d}(0,\varepsilon_{N})\right)
and aim to approximate
\Phi_{G_{i}}\left(B_{d}(0,\varepsilon_{N})\right)
. Further choosing
\varepsilon_{N}=\left(C_{d,\beta}\frac{p^{2}_{\max}}{p^{3}_{\min}}\frac{\log N}{N-1}\right)^{\frac{1}{d}}>\left(\frac{\beta C_{d}}{p_{\min}}\frac{\log N}{N}\right)^{1/d}
where
C_{d,\beta}
large enough, following
[
2
]
we consider a local polynomial estimator of
\Phi_{G_{i}}
of the following form. For
G_{i}\in\mathcal{G}
we define
\mathcal{V}_{i}=\{G_{j}-G_{i}\big|\left\lVert G_{j}-G_{i}\right\rVert\leq\varepsilon_{N}\}
, and consider a solution of the following minimization problem

P^{*}_{i},\{a^{*}_{i,S}\}_{2\leq\left|S\right|<\beta}\in\argmin_{P,a_{S}\leq\varepsilon_{n}^{-1}}\sum_{v_{j}\in\mathcal{V}_{i}}\left\lVert v_{j}-PP^{T}v_{j}-\sum_{S}a_{S}(P^{T}v_{j})^{S}\right\rVert^{2}.

(5.1)

Here
\argmin
is taken over all linear isometric embeddings
P:\mathbb{R}^{d}\mapsto\mathbb{R}^{D}
and all vectors
a_{S}\in\mathbb{R}^{D}
that satisfy
\left\lVert a_{S}\right\rVert\leq\varepsilon_{N}^{-1}
, where
S
is a multi-index
S=(S_{1},\dots,S_{d})
such that
2\leq\sum S_{k}\leq\beta
. Then, in the neighborhood of
G_{i}
the manifold is approximated as
M^{*}_{i}=\Phi_{i}^{*}\left(B_{d}(0,\varepsilon_{N})\right)
where

\Phi_{i}^{*}(z)=G_{i}+P^{*}_{i}z+\sum a^{*}_{i,S}z^{S},

(5.2)

where for
z\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and a multi-index
S
,
z^{S}=\prod_{j}z_{j}^{S_{j}}
. Note that the
\Phi_{i}^{*}
are not used to construct the estimator, which depends merely on the projections
P_{\mathcal{H}_{i}}
and are only used in the theoretical construction of an approximation of
s(t,\cdot)
for
t\in[T_{k},T_{k+1}]
by an element of
\mathcal{S}_{k}
.

Finally, we construct our support estimator as a union of polynomial surfaces
M^{*}=\bigcup M^{*}_{i}
.

Remark
.

[
2
]
optimize a slightly different functional, minimising over all projections
\pi
on
d
-dimensional subspaces and symmetric tensors
T_{j}:\operatorname{Im}\pi\mapsto\mathbb{R}^{D}
of order less than
\beta
. This is equivalent to (
5.1
) since the operators
P_{i}
parameterize the choice of the basis in
\operatorname{Im}\pi
and
\pi=P_{i}P^{T}_{i}
. Finally, the choice of the basis establishes a one-to-one correspondence between tensors of order
k
and homogeneous polynomials of degree
k
.

The affine subspace
G_{i}+\operatorname{Im}P^{*}_{i}
is a tangent space
T_{G_{i}}M^{*}
. Let
\pr_{i}
and
\pr^{*}_{i}
denote the projections on
T_{G_{i}}M-G_{i}
and
T_{G_{i}}M^{*}-G_{i}
respectively.
[
2
, Theorem 2]
states that the angle between subspaces
\angle\left(T_{G_{i}}M,T_{G_{i}}M^{*}\right):=\left\lVert\pr^{*}_{i}-\pr_{i}\right\rVert_{op}\lesssim\varepsilon_{N}^{\beta-1}
.

We restate this result as a bound on
P^{*}_{i}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\left(T_{G_{i}}M^{*}-G_{i}\right)
and show that it is close to the linear map
P_{i}:=\pr_{i}P^{*}_{i}\left(\left(P^{*}_{i}\right)^{T}\pr_{i}P^{*}_{i}\right)^{-1/2}
corresponding to the embedding
P_{i}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\left(T_{G_{i}}M-G_{i}\right)
.

Proposition 5.2
.

With probability
1-N^{-\beta/d}
for all
i\leq N
, the maps
P_{i}
are well-defined linear isometric embeddings satisfying
\left\lVert P^{*}_{i}-P_{i}\right\rVert_{op}\lesssim\varepsilon_{N}^{\beta-1}
.

Proposition
5.2
is proved in Appendix C.1.

Using the map
P_{i}
we identify
\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
T_{G_{i}}M
and set
\Phi_{i}=\Phi_{G_{i}}:B_{d}\left(0,r_{0}\right)\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow M
as an inverse of a projection map
P^{T}_{i}\circ\pr_{i}:M\mapsto\mathbb{R}^{d}
, in particular, this means that
\Phi_{G_{i}}(z)=G_{i}+P_{i}z+O\left(\left\lVert z\right\rVert^{2}\right)
.

The following statement is a generalization of
[
15
]
, Lemma A.2 and is proved in Appendix C.1.

Lemma 5.3
.

There is a large enough constant
L^{*}
, that does not depend on
D
, such that for any
\varepsilon_{N}<r_{0}/4
with probability
1-N^{-\frac{\beta}{d}}
for all
y_{i}
, all
0\leq k<\beta
and
z\in B_{d}\left(0,8\varepsilon_{N}\right)

\left\lVert\nabla^{k}\Phi_{i}(z)-\nabla^{k}\Phi^{*}_{i}(z)\right\rVert_{op}\leq L^{*}\varepsilon_{N}^{\beta-k}.

In particular
\left\lVert y-y^{*}\right\rVert\lesssim\varepsilon_{N}^{\beta}
and
{\angle\left(T_{y^{*}}M^{*},T_{y}M\right)\lesssim\varepsilon_{N}^{\beta-1}}
for
{y=\Phi_{i}(z)\in M,y^{*}=\Phi^{*}_{i}(z)\in M^{*}}
.

As a consequence, combined with Proposition
5.1
, the Hausdorff distance

d_{H}\left(M,M^{*}\right)=\max\left(\sup_{y\in M}\dist(y,M^{*}),\sup_{y^{*}\in M^{*}}\dist(y^{*},M)\right)\lesssim\varepsilon_{N}^{\beta}.

Note that
\varepsilon_{N}=\left(C_{d,\beta}\frac{p^{2}_{\max}}{p^{3}_{\min}}\frac{\log N}{N-1}\right)^{\frac{1}{d}}
, implying that
2d\log\varepsilon^{-1}_{N}\geq\log N
if
N
large enough. Together with Lemma
5.3
this shows that the constructed approximation satisfies Definition
2
.

Let us take a closer look at the solution of the optimization problem (
5.1
). When
\dim\spn\mathcal{V}_{i}\leq d

P_{i}P_{i}^{T}v=v
for all
v\in\mathcal{V}_{i}
, while when
\dim\spn\mathcal{V}_{i}>d
we claim that we can reduce the problem to a low-dimensional
\spn\mathcal{V}_{i}
. The following propositions justify this.

Proposition 5.4
.

If
\dim\spn\mathcal{V}_{i}>d
, then there is a solution
P_{i}^{*},a^{*}_{i,S}
of (
5.1
) such that
\operatorname{Im}P^{*}_{i}\subset\spn\mathcal{V}_{i}
and
a^{*}_{i,S}\in\Ker P_{i}^{*}\cap\spn\mathcal{V}_{i}
.

Proposition 5.5
.

There is a large enough constant
C_{\dim}
that does not depend on
D
such that with probability
1-N^{-\frac{\beta}{d}}
for all
y\in M
the size of the set

\mathcal{V}_{y}=\{G_{i}:\left\lVert G_{i}-y\right\rVert\leq\varepsilon_{N}\}

is bounded by
\left|\mathcal{V}_{y}\right|\leq C_{\dim}\log N.

In particular for all
G_{i}\in\mathcal{G}
holds

\left|\mathcal{V}_{i}\right|\leq C_{\dim}\log N.

The above two propositions are proved in Appendix C.1.

Together these statements show that with probability
1-N^{-\beta/d}
there is a piece-wise polynomial surface
M^{*}=\bigcup_{i=1}^{N}M^{*}_{i}
approximating
M
with error
\varepsilon_{N}^{\beta}\simeq\left(\log N/N\right)^{\beta/d}
such that each polynomial piece
M^{*}_{i}
lies in affine subspace
G_{i}+\spn\mathcal{V}_{i}
where
\mathcal{V}_{i}=\{G_{j}-G_{i}\big|\left\lVert G_{j}-G_{i}\right\rVert\leq\varepsilon_{N}\}
which is at most
C_{\dim}\log N
dimensional.

Finally, we recall that by taking a partition
\mu=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mu_{i}
of the measure
\mu
subordinated to the cover
M=\bigcup_{i=1}^{N}M_{i}
we construct the measure
\mu^{*}=\mu^{*}_{i}
where
\mu^{*}_{i}
is the pushforward of
\mu_{i}
under
\Phi^{*}_{i}\circ\Phi^{-1}_{i}
. Moreover, the functions
\Phi^{*}_{i}\circ\Phi^{-1}_{i}
provide transport maps which by Lemma
5.3
guarantees that
W_{2}(\mu,\mu^{*})\leq L^{*}\varepsilon^{\beta}_{N}
.

5.2
Manifold Approximation and the Score Function

In this section, we answer the second and third questions raised at the beginning of Section
5
.

To control the score matching error between
s^{*}(t,x)
and
s(t,x)
, we use a general result bounding the score-matching loss between two compactly supported distributions.

Proposition 5.6
.

Let
P,Q
be arbitrary compactly supported measures s.t.
W_{2}(P,Q)<\infty
, where
W_{2}
is the quadratic Wasserstein distance.

Letting
X\sim P
,
Y\sim Q
,
we write
X_{t}=c_{t}X+\sigma_{t}Z_{D}
, and
Y_{t}=c_{t}Y+\sigma_{t}Z_{D}
, where
Z_{D}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,Id)
be two standard Ornstein-Uhlenbeck processes initialised from
X,Y
respectively. These random variables are absolutely continuous w.r.t. the Lebesgue measure with densities
p(t,x),q(t,x)
respectively.

If we consider
\nabla\log q(t,x)
as an estimator of
s(t,x)=\nabla\log p(t,x)
on the interval
t\in[t_{\min},t_{\max}]
, then the corresponding score matching loss is bounded by

\int_{t_{\min}}^{t_{\max}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\|\nabla\log p(t,x)-\nabla\log q(t,x)\|^{2}p(t,x)dxdt\leq W^{2}_{2}(P,Q)\frac{c^{2}_{t_{\min}}}{4\sigma^{2}_{t_{\min}}}.

Proof.

We believe that this statement can be traced back at least to
[
42
]
, however, we could not find it in this form. Therefore, we provide a proof in Appendix C.2.∎

Remark
.

The bound is tight, to see this take
P=\delta_{0}
, and
Q=\delta_{x}
, for
\left\lVert x\right\rVert=\varepsilon
.

In Section
5.1
we constructed a measure
\mu^{*}
satisfying
W_{2}(\mu,\mu^{*})\leq L^{*}\varepsilon^{\beta}_{N}
, so if we denote as
s^{*}
the score function corresponding to
\mu^{*}
by Proposition
5.6

\int_{t_{\min}}^{t_{\max}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\|s(t,x)-s^{*}(t,x)\|^{2}p(t,x)dxdt\leq L^{*}\varepsilon_{N}^{2\beta}\frac{c^{2}_{t_{\min}}}{4\sigma^{2}_{t_{\min}}}.

Finally, we answer the last question and discuss how the substitution of the true score by the approximation
s^{*}(t,x)
affects the regularity bounds on the score function derived in the Section
4
. Let us present the bound on the correlation between Gaussian noise
Z_{D}
and the correction brought by the substitution of
M
by
M^{*}

Proposition 5.7
.

Let
M^{*}
be a piece-wise polynomial approximation satisfying Definition
2
. Then for all positive
\delta<1
with probability at least
1-\delta
for all
i\leq N
and
y\in M_{i}

\left|\left\langle Z_{D},y-\Phi_{i}^{*}\circ\Phi_{i}^{-1}(y)\right\rangle\right|\leq L^{*}\varepsilon_{N}^{\beta}\sqrt{2d\log\varepsilon^{-1}_{N}+2\log 2\delta^{-1}}.

The proof is provided in Appendix C.2.

All the bounds in Section
4
follow from Proposition
4.1
, therefore the statements presented in the previous section still hold if
t\geq\varepsilon^{2\beta}_{N}
in the case of
M^{*}
too, albeit with slightly different constants that are still independent of
D
. We present these results in Appendix C.

6
Wasserstein Convergence

In this section, we analyze the convergence under the
W_{1}
metric of the diffusion model that uses the estimator obtained in Theorem
3.1
and prove Theorem
3.2
. We prove a more general statement that does not depend on the specific construction of our estimator and works for any estimator satisfying the assumptions below.

{Assumption}

There exist constants
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{denoiser}}>0
,
C_{\mathrm{tail}}\geq 1
such that the
score function estimate
\hat{s}
verifies:

(i)

\hat{s}
has a continuous denoising loss bounded by
\varepsilon^{2}_{\mathrm{denoiser}}
, i.e.

\int_{\underline{T}}^{\overline{T}}\sigma_{t}^{2}\mathbb{E}\|\hat{s}(t,X_{t})-s(t,X_{t})\|^{2}\,dt\leq\varepsilon_{\mathrm{denoiser}}^{2},

(6.1)

(ii)

for every
t\in[\underline{T},\overline{T}]
and some
\eta>0

\hat{s}
satisfies the following high probability bound

\rP\left(\|\sigma_{t}\hat{s}(t,X_{t})+Z_{D}\|>C_{\mathrm{tail}}\log\underline{T}^{-1}\sqrt{C_{W}+\log\eta^{-1}}\right)\leq\eta,

(6.2)

for some
C_{\mathrm{tail}},C_{W}>0

Under this assumption, we show the following.

Theorem 6.1
.

Assume that measure
\mu
satisfies the same assumptions as in Theorem
3.1
, let
s
be the score function corresponding to
\mu
and
\hat{s}
be its approximation satisfying Assumption
6
with
\eta=O\left(\varepsilon^{-4}_{\mathrm{denoiser}}\underline{T}^{-1}\left(\log\underline{T}+\overline{T}\right)\right)
.

Then, there exists a discretization scheme requiring
L=O\left(\varepsilon^{-2}_{\mathrm{denoiser}}\left(\log\underline{T}+\overline{T}\right)\right)
steps to generate one sample from a measure
\hat{\mu}
which satisfies

\displaystyle W_{1}(\mu,\hat{\mu})\lesssim\sqrt{D}e^{-\overline{T}}+\sqrt{\underline{T}}\log\underline{T}^{-1}\sqrt{C_{W}+\log(\varepsilon^{-1}_{\mathrm{denoiser}}\cdot\underline{T}^{-1}\cdot\overline{T})}

(6.3)

\displaystyle\quad+\varepsilon_{\mathrm{denoiser}}\cdot\log(\overline{T}\cdot\underline{T}^{-1})\log\underline{T}^{-1}\sqrt{C_{W}+\log(\varepsilon^{-1}_{\mathrm{denoiser}}\cdot\underline{T}^{-1}\cdot\overline{T})}\sqrt{(d\log\underline{T}^{-1}+C_{W})(\log{\underline{T}^{-1}}+\overline{T})}

In the rest of the section, we prove Theorem
6.1
. In Section
6.1
we construct the discretization mesh, in Section
6.2
we introduce the discretization scheme, and in Section
6.3
we prove Theorem
6.1
for this specific discretization scheme.

6.1
Randomized Discretization Mesh

In this section, we construct a randomized discretization mesh that inherits certain useful properties of those used in
[
31
]
and
[
7
]
. The randomization is needed to ensure that the denoising loss evaluated at the grid points can be controlled by its continuous version (
6.1
).

Following
[
7
]
we introduce
\kappa<1/4
, which we fix later. We consider the same grid as in Section
3.1
with

\overline{T}:=T_{K}>T_{K-1}>\cdots>T_{0}:=\underline{T}
, a crude dyadic partition
defined by
T_{k+1}=2T_{k}
and without loss of generality we assume that
\log(\overline{T}/\underline{T})\in\log_{2}\mathbb{N}
.

Figure 1:
Illustration of constructed partition. The interval
[\underline{T},\overline{T}]
is first split into dyadic blocks
[T_{k},T_{k+1}]
(red), and each block is split into smaller blocks of size
\Delta_{k}:=(\kappa/4)\min(1,T_{k})
with a random indent
u_{k}\Delta_{k}
, where
u_{k}\sim U[0,1]
.

For each block
[T_{k},T_{k+1}]
, let

\Delta_{k}:=(\kappa/4)\min(1,T_{k})
, sample
u_{k}\sim U[0,1]

independently and define

\tau_{k,1}:=T_{k+1}-\Delta_{k}u_{k},\qquad\tau_{k,\ell}:=\tau_{k,1}-(\ell-1)\Delta_{k},\qquad\ell\geq 2.

Letting
L_{k}:=\max\{\ell\geq 1:\tau_{k,\ell}>T_{k}\}
, the points
\{\tau_{k,\ell}\}_{k\leq K,\ell\leq L_{k}}
will form the discretization grid that we will work with. We write

\overline{T}>\tau_{(1)}>\cdots>\tau_{(L)}>\underline{T},

for
the grid points listed in decreasing order, where

L:=\sum_{k=0}^{K-1}L_{k}
is their overall
number.
We also define
i_{k}:=\{i:\tau_{(i)}=\tau_{K-k,1}\},k\leq K
, the index of the largest grid point contained in the interval
[T_{K-k},T_{K-k+1}]
and
i_{K}:=L
.

We define
\gamma_{i}=\tau_{(i)}-\tau_{(i+1)}
and introduce the score matching error corresponding to the constructed grid

\varepsilon^{2}_{denoiser}(u):=\sum_{i=1}^{L-1}\gamma_{i}\sigma^{2}_{\tau_{(i)}}\mathbb{E}\left\lVert\hat{s}(\tau_{(i)},X_{\tau_{(i)}})-s(\tau_{(i)},X_{\tau_{(i)}})\right\rVert^{2}

The following proposition summarizes the properties of the constructed partition:

Proposition 6.2
.

Let
\{\tau_{(1)},\dots,\tau_{(L)}\}
be the random grid constructed above. Then we have the following:

(i)

the condition introduced in
[
7
, Page 5]
holds, i.e.

\tau_{(i)}-\tau_{(i+1)}\leq\kappa\min(1,\tau_{(i+1)}),\quad 0\leq i\leq M-1;

(ii)

\tau_{(1)}>\overline{T}-\kappa,\quad\tau_{(L)}<(1+\kappa)\underline{T},\quad\tau_{(i_{k})}/\tau_{(i_{k+1})}<4;

(iii)

The total number of elements
L
does not exceed

L\leq K-1+\frac{4}{\kappa}\left(\overline{T}+\left\lceil\log_{2}\underline{T}^{-1}\right\rceil\right)=O\left(\kappa^{-1}(\log{\underline{T}^{-1}}+\overline{T})\right),and

(6.4)

(iv)

the discretised denoiser loss evaluated at the grid points
\tau_{(i)}
can be controlled using (
6.1
).

\mathbb{E}_{u}\varepsilon^{2}_{denoiser}(u)=\mathbb{E}_{u}\sum_{i=1}^{L-1}\gamma_{i}\sigma^{2}_{\tau_{(i)}}\mathbb{E}\left\lVert\hat{s}(\tau_{(i)},X_{\tau_{(i)}})-s(\tau_{(i)},X_{\tau_{(i)}})\right\rVert^{2}\\
\leq 2\int_{\underline{T}}^{\overline{T}}\sigma^{2}_{t}\mathbb{E}\left\lVert\hat{s}(t,X_{t})-s(t,X_{t})\right\rVert^{2}dt\leq 2\varepsilon^{2}_{\mathrm{denoiser}}.

(6.5)

Finally, we define the backward discretization times
0=t_{1}<t_{2}<\ldots<t_{L+1}=\tau_{(1)}
and
t_{i}=\tau_{(1)}-\tau_{(i)},i\leq L
, and use them to define the discrete backward process in the next section.

6.2
Modification of Discretization Scheme

Let us introduce

R_{t}(\eta):=\min\left(1,C_{\mathrm{tail}}(\sigma_{t}/c_{t})\log\underline{T}^{-1}\sqrt{C_{W}+\log\eta^{-1}}\right).

(6.6)

We recall, that by Assumption
6
with probability at least
1-\eta
the estimator
\hat{e}
satisfies

\displaystyle\left\lVert\hat{e}(t,X_{t})-X_{0}\right\rVert

\displaystyle\leq R_{t}(\eta),

(6.7)

in particular, implying that for any
s,t>0
, with probability at least
1-\eta

\displaystyle\left\lVert\hat{e}(t,X_{t})-\hat{e}(s,X_{s})\right\rVert

\displaystyle\leq 2R_{\max(s,t)}(\eta/2).

(6.8)

We propose the following modification of the score function which makes it self-consistent. Along the discretized trajectory
(y_{t_{1}},y_{t_{2}},\ldots,y_{t_{L}})
we collect the observations

(\hat{e}(\tau_{(1)},y_{t_{1}}),\hat{e}(\tau_{(2)},y_{t_{2}}),\ldots,\hat{e}(\tau_{(i)},y_{t_{i}}))
and we construct a modified estimator
\bar{e}(\tau_{(i)},y_{t_{1}:t_{i}})
, where
y_{t_{1}:t_{i}}=(y_{t_{1}},\ldots,y_{t_{i}})
for all
i\leq L
, which satisfies (
6.8
) almost surely instead of with high probability. Choosing
\eta=\underline{T}\delta/L^{2}
in
6.8
we define
the estimator of interest as:

\bar{e}(\tau_{(i)},y_{t_{1}:t_{i}}):=\begin{cases}\hat{e}(\tau_{(1)},y_{t_{1}})&\text{ if }i=1,\\
\hat{e}(\tau_{(i)},y_{t_{i}})&\text{ if }\forall j<i:\left\lVert\bar{e}(\tau_{(j)},y_{t_{1}:t_{j}})-\hat{e}(\tau_{(i)},y_{t_{i}})\right\rVert\leq 2R_{\tau_{(j)}}\left(\frac{\underline{T}\delta}{2L^{2}}\right),\\
\bar{e}(\tau_{(i-1)},y_{t_{1}:t_{i-1}})&\text{ otherwise}.\end{cases}

(6.9)

That is, at each step if according to (
6.8
) the new observation
\hat{e}(\tau_{(i)},y_{t_{i}})
is an outlier, we discard it and use the previous estimate
\bar{e}(\tau_{(i-1)},y_{t_{1}:t_{i-1}})
.

This construction guarantees that for all
j<i

\left\lVert\bar{e}(\tau_{(i)},y_{t_{1}:t_{i}})-\bar{e}(\tau_{(j)},y_{t_{1}:t_{j}})\right\rVert\leq 2R_{\tau_{(j)}}(\eta/2).

Since
\eta=\underline{T}\delta/L^{2}
, in (
6.8
),
the event

\displaystyle\bigg\{\left\lVert\hat{e}({\tau_{(i)}},X_{\tau_{(i)}})-\hat{e}({\tau_{(j)}},X_{\tau_{(j)}})\right\rVert

\displaystyle\leq 2R_{\tau_{(j)}}\left(\frac{\underline{T}\delta}{2L^{2}}\right),\qquad\text{for all pairs $j<i$}\bigg\}

(6.10)

has probability at least
1-\underline{T}\delta/L^{2}
for every
i
.

In particular, with probability at least
1-\underline{T}\delta/L
for all
i\leq L
simultaneously
\hat{e}(\tau_{(i)},X_{\tau_{(i)}})=\bar{e}(\tau_{(i)},X_{\tau_{(i)}:\tau_{(1)}})
, with
X_{\tau_{(i)}:\tau_{(1)}}=(X_{\tau_{(i)}},\ldots,X_{\tau_{(1)}})
. Note that the above probability is conditional on
\tau_{(1)},\cdots,\tau_{(L)}
which are random.

Substituting the bound above, and using that
\left\lVert\bar{e}\right\rVert\leq\left\lVert\hat{e}\right\rVert\leq C_{1}
, a.s., we can bound the discretized denoising loss corresponding to

\bar{s}(\tau_{(i)},x_{\tau_{(i)}:\tau_{(1)}})=[c_{t}\bar{e}(\tau_{(i)},x_{\tau_{(i)}:\tau_{(1)}})-x_{\tau_{(i)}}]/\sigma^{2}_{\tau(i)}

as

\mathbb{E}_{u}\sum_{i=1}^{L-1}\gamma_{i}\sigma^{2}_{\tau_{(i)}}\mathbb{E}\left\lVert\bar{s}(\tau_{(i)},X_{\tau_{(i)}:\tau_{(1)}})-s(\tau_{(i)},X_{\tau_{(i)}})\right\rVert^{2}\\
\leq\mathbb{E}_{u}\sum_{i=1}^{L}\left(\gamma_{i}\sigma^{2}_{\tau_{(i)}}\mathbb{E}\left\lVert\hat{s}(\tau_{(i)},X_{\tau_{(i)}})-s(\tau_{(i)},X_{\tau_{(i)}})\right\rVert^{2}\right)\\
+\mathbb{E}_{u}\left(\mathbb{P}\left[\exists i\leq L;\hat{e}(\tau_{(i)},X_{\tau_{(i)}})\neq\bar{e}(\tau_{(i)},X_{\tau_{(i)}:\tau_{(1)}})|\tau_{(1)},\cdots,\tau_{(L)}\right]\sum_{i=1}^{L}\frac{\gamma_{i}c_{\tau_{(i)}}}{\sigma^{2}_{\tau_{(i)}}}\right)\\
\leq\mathbb{E}_{u}\sum_{i=0}^{L}\left(\gamma_{i}\sigma^{2}_{\tau_{(i)}}\mathbb{E}\left\lVert\hat{s}(\tau_{(i)},X_{\tau_{(i)}})-s(\tau_{(i)},X_{\tau_{(i)}})\right\rVert^{2}+{\sigma^{-2}_{\tau_{(i)}}\frac{\underline{T}\delta}{L}}\right)\leq 2(\varepsilon^{2}_{\mathrm{denoiser}}+\delta),

where to get the last inequality we used that
\sigma^{2}_{\tau_{(i)}}\geq\tau_{(i)}\geq\underline{T}
.

Following
[
33
]
, we consider the
DDPM discretization over the interval
[0,\tau_{(1)}]

\begin{cases}\bar{Y}_{t_{i+1}}\mid\bar{Y}_{t_{1}:t_{i}}=c_{\gamma_{i}}^{-1}\bar{Y}_{t_{i}}+\frac{\sigma_{\gamma_{i}}^{2}}{c_{\gamma_{i}}}\bar{s}(\tau_{(i)},\bar{Y}_{t_{1}:t_{i}})+\sigma_{\gamma_{i}}\frac{\sigma_{\tau_{(i+1)}}}{\sigma_{\tau_{(i)}}}Z_{i},Z_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i.i.d.}}{{\sim}}\mathcal{N}(0,\Id_{D}),\quad\quad i<L,\\
Y_{t_{L+1}}\mid\bar{Y}_{t_{1}:t_{L}}=\bar{e}(\tau_{(L)},\bar{Y}_{t_{1}:t_{L}}),\\
\bar{Y}_{0}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\Id_{D}).\end{cases}

(6.11)

As it is shown in
[
33
]
, the discretized process admits a
continuous interpolation on each interval
[t_{i},t_{i+1})
, for
i\leq M

\begin{cases}d\bar{Y}_{t}=\left[\bar{Y}_{t}+2\bar{s}(\tau_{(1)}-t,\bar{Y}_{t}\mid\tau_{(i)},\bar{Y}_{t_{1}:t_{i}})\right]\,dt+\sqrt{2}\,dB^{\prime}_{t},&t\in[t_{i},t_{i+1}),\\
\bar{Y}_{0}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\Id_{D}),\end{cases}

(6.12)

where for
t<\tau_{(i)}
,

\bar{s}(t,x_{t}\mid\tau_{(i)},x_{\tau_{(1)}:\tau_{(i)}})=c_{{\tau_{(i)}}-t}^{-1}\frac{\sigma_{\tau_{(i)}}^{2}}{\sigma_{t}^{2}}\bar{s}({\tau_{(i)}},x_{\tau_{(1)}:\tau_{(i)}})-\frac{x_{t}-c_{{\tau_{(i)}}-t}^{-1}x_{\tau_{(i)}}}{\sigma_{t}^{2}}=\frac{c_{t}\bar{e}({\tau_{(i)}},x_{\tau_{(1)}:\tau_{(i)}})-x_{t}}{\sigma^{2}_{t}}.

Equivalently, once the process reaches time
t_{i}
, the next step simulates for time
\gamma_{i}
the
Brownian bridge on
[t_{i},\tau_{(1)}]
connecting
\bar{Y}_{t_{i}}
and
\bar{e}(\tau_{(i)},\bar{Y}_{t_{1}:t_{i}})
.

6.3
W_{1}
Convergence

We are now ready to prove Theorem
6.1
.
We recall that by construction, the indices

i_{k},k\leq K+1

satisfy
\tau_{(i_{k})}/\tau_{(i_{k+1})}<4
. Following
